Taylor expansion in linear logic is invertible
Daniel de Carvalho

Abstract
. Each Multiplicative Exponential Linear Logic (MELL) proof-net can be expanded into a differential net, which is its Taylor expansion. We prove that two different MELL proof-nets have two different Taylor expansions. As a corollary, we prove a completeness result for MELL: We show that the relational model is injective for MELL proof-nets, i.e. the equality between MELL proof-nets in the relational model is exactly axiomatized by cut-elimination.



Dec. 14, 2017Dec. 07, 2018

\psset
treemode=U,levelsep=5mm

In the seminal paper by Harvey Friedman
[
18
]
, it has been shown that equality between simply-typed lambda-terms in the full typed structure
\mathcal{M}_{X}
over an infinite set
X
is completely axiomatized by
\beta
and
\eta
: for any simply-typed lambda-terms
v
and
u
, we have
(\mathcal{M}_{X}\vDash v=u\Leftrightarrow v\simeq_{\beta\eta}u)
. Some variations, refinements and generalizations of this result have been provided by Gordon Plotkin
[
30
]
and Alex Simpson
[
33
]
. A natural problem is to know whether a similar result could be obtained for Linear Logic.

Such a result can be seen as a “separation” theorem. To obtain such separation theorems, it is a prerequisite to have a “canonical” syntax.
When Jean-Yves Girard introduced Linear Logic (LL)
[
19
]
, he not only introduced a sequent calculus system but also “proof-nets”. Indeed, as for LJ and LK (sequent calculus systems for intuitionnistic and classical logic, respectively), different proofs in LL sequent calculus can represent “morally” the same proof: proof-nets were introduced to find a unique representative for these proofs.

The technology of proof-nets was completely satisfactory for the multiplicative fragment without units.
1
1

1

For the multiplicative fragment with units, it has been recently shown
[
21
,
22
]
that, in some sense, no satisfactory notion of proof-net can exist. Our proof-nets have no jump, so they identify too many sequent calculus proofs, but not more than the relational semantics.
For proof-nets having additives, contractions or weakenings, it was easy to exhibit different proof-nets that should be identified. Despite some flaws, the discovery of proof-nets was striking. In particular, Vincent Danos proved by syntactical means in
[
6
]
the confluence of these proof-nets for the Multiplicative Exponential Linear Logic fragment (MELL). For additives, the problem to have a satisfactory notion of proof-net has been addressed in
[
23
]
. For MELL, a “new syntax” was introduced in
[
7
]
. In the original syntax, the following properties of the weakening and of the contraction did not hold:

•

the associativity of the contraction;

•

the neutrality of the weakening for the contraction;

•

the contraction and the weakening as morphisms of co-algebras.

But they hold in the new syntax; at least for MELL, we got a syntax that was a good candidate to deserve being considered “canonical”. Then trying to prove that any two (
\eta
-expanded) MELL proof-nets that are equal in some denotational semantics are
\beta
-joinable has become sensible and had at least the two following motivations:

•

to prove the canonicity of the “new syntax” (if we quotient more normal proof-nets, then we would identify proof-nets having different semantics);

•

to prove the confluence by semantic means (if a proof-net reduces to two cut-free proof-nets, then they have the same semantics, so they would be
\beta
-joinable, hence equal).

The problem of
injectivity
2
2

2

The tradition of the lambda-calculus community rather suggests the word “completeness” and the terminology of category theory rather suggests the word “faithfulness”, but we follow here the tradition of the Linear Logic community.
of the denotational semantics for MELL, which is the question whether equality in the denotational semantics between (
\eta
-expanded) MELL proof-nets is exactly axiomatized by cut-elimination or not, can be seen as a study of the separation property with a semantic approach. The first work on the study of this property in the framework of proof-nets is
[
25
]
where the
authors deal with the translation into LL of the pure
\lambda
-calculus; it has been studied more recently for the intuitionistic multiplicative
fragment of LL
[
26
]
and for differential nets
[
27
]
. For Parigot’s
\lambda\mu
-calculus, see
[
8
]
and
[
32
]
.

Finally the precise problem of injectivity for MELL has been addressed by Lorenzo Tortora de Falco in his PhD thesis
[
34
]
and in
[
35
]
for the (multiset based) coherence semantics and the multiset based relational semantics.
He gave partial results and counter-examples for the coherence semantics: the (multiset based) coherence semantics is not injective for MELL.
Also, it was conjectured that the relational model is injective for MELL. It is worth mentioning that the injectivity of the relational model trivially entails the injectivity of other denotational semantics: non-uniform coherence semantics (
[
4
]
and
[
3
]
), finiteness spaces
[
15
]
, weighted sets
[
1
]
…

We presented an abstract of a proof of this conjecture in
[
10
]
. This result can be seen as

•

a semantic separation property in the sense of
[
18
]
;

•

a semantic proof of the confluence property;

•

a proof of the “canonicity” of the new syntax of MELL proof-nets;

•

a proof of the fact that if the Taylor expansions of two cut-free MELL proof-nets into differential nets coincide, then the two proof-nets coincide.

Differential proof-nets
[
17
]
are linear approximations of proof-nets that are meant to allow the expression of the Taylor expansion of proof-nets as infinite series of their
linear approximations, which can be seen as a syntactic counterpart of quantitative semantics of Linear Logic (see
[
16
]
for an introduction to the topic). Now, in the present paper, we not only provide a fully detailed proof of this result, we also prove a more general result: We show that if the Taylor expansions of
any
two MELL proof-nets into differential nets coincide, then the two proof-nets coincide, i.e. we removed the assumption of the absence of cuts. Then the injectivity of the relational semantics becomes a corollary of this new result. By the way, the proof is essentially the same as before.
3
3

3

The two main differences are the following ones:

(1)

The
pseudo-experiments
we consider are not necessarily induced by
experiments
any more, which means that we consider simple differential nets that might reduce to
0
and have no counterpart in the denotational semantics.

(2)

The constraints on the basis
k
of the
k
-heterogeneous pseudo-experiments
we consider are stronger.

In
[
35
]
, a proof of the injectivity of the relational model is given for a weak fragment.
But despite many efforts (
[
34
]
,
[
35
]
,
[
2
]
,
[
28
]
,
[
27
]
,
[
29
]
…), all
the attempts to prove the conjecture failed up to now.
New progress was made in
[
13
]
, where it has been proved that the relational semantics is injective for “connected” MELL proof-nets. Even though, there, “connected” is understood as a very strong assumption, the set of “connected” MELL proof-nets contains the fragment of MELL defined by removing weakenings and units. Actually
[
13
]
proved a much stronger result: in the full MELL fragment, two cut-free proof-nets
R
and
R^{\prime}
with the same
interpretation are the same up to the map associating auxiliary doors with their box (we say that they have the same LPS
4
4

4

The LPS of a cut-free proof-net is the graph obtained by forgetting the outline of the boxes but keeping the trace of the auxiliary doors. The acronym LPS originally stands for “Linear Proof-Structure”; this terminology might be misleading since the LPS is much more informative than the result of an injective
1
-experiment but is well-established, so we keep the acronym forgetting what it stood for.
- for instance, there are exactly four different proof-nets whose LPS is the LPS depicted in Figure
22
, p.
22
: These four proof-nets are the ones depicted in Figure
26
, Figure
26
, Figure
26
and Figure
26
, p.
26
). We wrote: “This result can be expressed in terms of differential nets: two cut-free proof-nets with different LPS have different Taylor expansions. We also believe this work is an essential step
towards the proof of the full conjecture.” Despite the fact we obtained a very interesting result about
all
the proof-nets (i.e. also for non-‘‘connected’’ proof-nets
5
5

5

and even adding the MIX rule
), the last sentence was a bit too optimistic, since, in the present paper, which presents a proof of the full conjecture, we could not use any previous result nor any previous technique/idea.

Let us give one more interpretation of its significance. First, notice that a proof of this result should consist in showing that, given two non
\beta
-equivalent proof-nets
R
and
R^{\prime}
, their respective semantics
\llbracket R\rrbracket
and
\llbracket R^{\prime}\rrbracket
are not equal, i.e.
\llbracket R\rrbracket\setminus\llbracket R^{\prime}\rrbracket\not=\emptyset
or
\llbracket R^{\prime}\rrbracket\setminus\llbracket R\rrbracket\not=\emptyset
.
6
6

6

The converse, i.e. two
\beta
-equivalent proof-nets have the same semantics, holds by soundness.
But, actually, we prove something much stronger: We prove that, given a proof-net
R
, there exist two points
\alpha
and
\beta
such that, for any proof-net
R^{\prime}
, we have
(\{\alpha,\beta\}\subseteq\llbracket R^{\prime}\rrbracket\Leftrightarrow R\simeq_{\beta}R^{\prime})
.

Now, the points of the relational model can be seen as non-idempotent intersection types
7
7

7

Idempotency of intersection (
\alpha\cap\alpha=\alpha
) does not hold.
(see
[
9
]
and
[
11
]
for a correspondence between points of the relational model and System R - System R has also been studied recently in
[
5
]
). And the proof given in the present paper uses MELL types only to derive the normalization property; actually we prove the injectivity for cut-free proof-nets in an untyped framework:
8
8

8

Our proof even works for “non-correct” proof-structures (correctness is the property characterizing nets corresponding in a typed framework with proofs in sequent calculus): we could expect that if the injectivity of the relational semantics holds for proof-nets corresponding with MELL sequent calculus, then it still holds for proof-nets corresponding with MELL+MIX sequent calculus, since the category
Rel
of sets and relations is a compact closed category. The paper
[
20
]
assuming correctness substituted in the proof the “bridges” of
[
13
]
, which are essentially connected components (in the strong sense of the term since the notion of
bridge
ignores boxes - we will consider other “connected components” in our proof), by “empires”, which, in contrast, discriminate between the connectors
\otimes
and
\invamp
.
Substituting the assumption that proof-nets are typed by the assumption that proof-nets are normalizable does not change anything to the proof.
9
9

9

Except that we have to consider the
atomic
subset of the interpretation instead of the full interpretation (see Definition
4.1
).
In
[
12
]
, we gave a semantic characterization of normalizable untyped proof-nets and we characterized “head-normalizable” proof-nets as proof-nets having a non-empty interpretation in the relational semantics, while
[
14
]
gave a characterization of strongly normalizable untyped proof-nets
via
non-idempotent intersection types. Principal typings in untyped
\lambda
-calculus are intersection types which allow to recover all the intersection types of some term. If, for instance, we consider the System
R
of
[
9
]
and
[
11
]
, it is enough to consider some
injective
1
-point
10
10

10

An
injective
k
-point
is a point in which all the positive multisets have cardinality
k
and in which each atom occurring in it occurs exactly twice.
to obtain the principal typing of an untyped
\lambda
-term. But, generally, for normalizable MELL proof-nets,
injective
k
-points
, for any
k
, are not principal typings; indeed, two cut-free MELL proof-nets having the same LPS have the same injective
k
-points for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
. In the current paper we show that a
1
-point and a
k
-heterogeneous point
11
11

11

k
-heterogeneous points
are points in which every positive multiset has cardinality
k^{j}
for some
j>0
and, for any
j>0
, there is at most one occurrence of a positive multiset having cardinality
k^{j}
(see our Definition
4.1
).
together allow to recover the interpretation of any normalizable MELL proof-net; by the way, our result cannot be improved in such a way that one point would be already enough for any MELL proof-net (see our Proposition
52
). So, our work can also be seen as a first attempt to find a right notion of “principal typing” of intersection types in Linear Logic. As a consequence, the introduction of technologies allowing to compute directly by semantic means this principal typing should make possible normalization by evaluation, as in
[
31
]
for
\lambda
-calculus; that said, the complexity of such a computation is still unclear.

Section
1
formalizes untyped proof-structures (PS’s) and typed proof-structures (typed PS’s). Taylor expansion is defined in Section
2
. Section
3
presents our algorithm leading from the Taylor expansion of
R
to the rebuilding of
R
and proves its correctness, which shows the invertibility of Taylor expansion (Corollary
51
). Section
4
is devoted to show the completeness (the injectivity) of the relational semantics: for any typed PS’s
R
and
R^{\prime}
, we have
(\llbracket R\rrbracket=\llbracket R^{\prime}\rrbracket\Leftrightarrow R\simeq_{\beta}R^{\prime})
(Corollary
74
), where
\simeq_{\beta}
is the reflexive symmetric transitive closure of the cut-elimination relation, by showing first that cut-free PS’s are characterized by their relational interpretation (Theorem
62
).

Notations
.

We denote by
\varepsilon
the empty sequence.

For any
n\geq 2
, for any
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n+1}
, we define, by induction on
n
, the
(n+1)
-tuple
(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n+1})
by setting
(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n+1})=(\alpha_{1},(\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\ldots,\alpha_{n+1}))
.

For any set
E
, we denote by
\mathfrak{P}(E)
the set of subsets of
E
, by
\mathfrak{P}_{\textit{fin}}(E)
the set of finite subsets of
E
and by
\mathfrak{P}_{2}(E)
the set
\{\mathcal{E}_{0}\in\mathfrak{P}(E);\mathsf{Card}\left(\mathcal{E}_{0}\right)=2\}
.

A multiset
f
of elements of some set
\mathcal{E}
is a function
\mathcal{E}\to\mathbb{N}
; we denote by
\textit{Supp}(f)

the support of
f
i.e. the set
\{e\in\mathcal{E};f(e)\not=0\}
. A multiset
f
is said to be
finite
if
\textit{Supp}(f)
is finite. The set of finite multisets of elements of some set
\mathcal{E}
is denoted by
\mathcal{M}_{\textit{fin}}(\mathcal{E})
.

If
f
is a function
\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
,
x_{0}\in\mathcal{E}
and
y\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
, then we denote by
f[x_{0}\mapsto y]
the function
\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
defined by
f[x_{0}\mapsto y](x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}f(x)&\textit{if $x\not=x_{0}$;}\\
y&\textit{if $x=x_{0}$.}\end{array}\right.
If
f
is a function
\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{E}_{0}\subseteq\mathsf{dom}(f)=\mathcal{E}
, then we denote by
f[\mathcal{E}_{0}]
the set
\{f(x);x\in\mathcal{E}_{0}\}
and by
f_{\ast}
the function
\mathfrak{P}(E)\to\mathfrak{P}(E)^{\prime}
that associates with every
\mathcal{E}_{0}\in\mathfrak{P}(E)
the set
f[\mathcal{E}_{0}]
.

1.
Syntax

1.1.
Differential proof-structures

We introduce the syntactical objects we are interested in. As recalled in the introduction, correctness does not play any role, that is why we do not restrict our nets to be correct and we rather consider proof-structures (
PS’s
). Since it is convenient to represent formally our proof using differential nets possibly with boxes (
differential PS’s
), we define PS’s as differential PS’s satisfying some conditions (Definition
1.1
). More generally,
differential in-PS’s
are defined by induction on the depth, which is the maximum level of box nesting: Definition
1.1
, Definition
1.1
and Definition
1.1
concern what happens at depth
0
, i.e. whenever there is no box; in particular, typed ground-structures allow to represent proofs of the multiplicative fragment (MLL).

We set
\mathfrak{T}=

\{\otimes,

\invamp,

1,\bot,\oc,\wn,\textit{ax}\}
.

{defi}

A
pre-net
is a
7
-tuple
\mathcal{G}=(\mathcal{P},l,\mathcal{W},\mathcal{A},

\mathcal{C},

t,\mathcal{L})
, where

•

\mathcal{P}
is a finite set; the elements of
\mathcal{P}
are the
ports of
\mathcal{G}
;

•

is a function
\mathcal{P}\to\mathfrak{T}
; the element

of
\mathfrak{T}
is the
label of
p
in
\mathcal{G}
;

•

\mathcal{W}
is a subset of
\mathcal{P}
; the elements of
\mathcal{W}
are the
wires of
\mathcal{G}
;
12
12

12

We identify a wire with its source port.

•

\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathfrak{P}_{2}(\mathcal{P})
is a partition of
\{p\in\mathcal{P};l(p)=\textit{ax}\}
; the elements of
\mathcal{A}
are the
axioms of
\mathcal{G}
;

•

\mathcal{C}
is a subset of
\mathfrak{P}_{2}(\mathcal{P}\setminus\mathcal{W})
such that
(\forall c,c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C})(c\cap c^{\prime}\not=\emptyset\Rightarrow c=c^{\prime})
; the elements of
\mathcal{C}
are the
cuts of
\mathcal{G}
;

•

t
is a function
\mathcal{W}\to\{p\in\mathcal{P};l(p)\notin\{1,\bot,\textit{ax}\}\}
such that, for any
p\in\mathcal{P}
, we have
(l(p)\in

\{\otimes,

\invamp\}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{Card}\left(\{w\in\mathcal{W};t(w)=p\}\right)=2)
;
if
t(w)=p
, then
w
is a
premise of
p
; the
arity
{\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{G}}(p)
of
p
is the number of its premises;

•

and
\mathcal{L}
is a subset of
\{w\in\mathcal{W};l(t(w))\in\{\otimes,\invamp\}\}
such that
(\forall p\in\mathcal{P})

(l(p)\in\{\otimes,\invamp\}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{Card}\left({\{w\in\mathcal{L};t(w)=p\}}\right)=1)
; if
w\in\mathcal{L}
such that
t(w)=p
, then
w
is
the left premise of
p
; if
w\in\mathcal{W}\setminus\mathcal{L}
such that
l(t(w))\in\{\otimes,\invamp\}
, then
w
is
the right premise of
t(w)
.

We set
\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{G})=\mathcal{W}
,
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{G})=\mathcal{P}
,
l_{\mathcal{G}}=l
,
t_{\mathcal{G}}=t
,
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G})=\mathcal{L}
,
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{G})=\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{G})=\mathcal{C}
. The set
\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{G})=\mathcal{P}\setminus(\mathcal{W}\cup\bigcup\mathcal{C})
is the set of
conclusions of
\mathcal{G}
. For any

, we set
\mathcal{P}^{t}(\mathcal{G})=\{p\in\mathcal{P};l(p)=t\}
; we set
\mathcal{P}^{\textit{m}}(\mathcal{G})=\mathcal{P}^{\otimes}(\mathcal{G})\cup\mathcal{P}^{\invamp}(\mathcal{G})

\mathcal{P}^{\textit{e}}(\mathcal{G})
of
exponential ports of
\mathcal{G}
is
\mathcal{P}^{\oc}(\mathcal{G})\cup\mathcal{P}^{\wn}(\mathcal{G})
.

A
pre-ground-structure
is a pre-net
\mathcal{G}
such that
\mathsf{im}(t_{\mathcal{G}})\cap\mathcal{P}^{\oc}(\mathcal{G})=\emptyset
.

Notice that, although we depict cuts as wires
13
13

13

like wires between principal ports in the formalism of interaction nets

(but, in contrast with interaction nets, Definition

. A wire

goes from a port that has the same name
p
to its target
t(p)
; instead of using arrows in our figures to indicate the direction, we will use the following convention: Unless

(but in this case there is no ambiguity since such a port

to some port

.

{defi}

Given a pre-net
\mathcal{G}
, we denote by
\leq_{\mathcal{G}}
the reflexive transitive closure of the binary relation
P_{\mathcal{G}}
on
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{G})
defined by
(P_{\mathcal{G}}(q,p)\Leftrightarrow t_{\mathcal{G}}(p)=q)
.

A
simple differential net
(resp. a
ground-structure
) is a pre-net
\mathcal{G}
(resp. a pre-ground-structure) such that the relation
P_{\mathcal{G}}
is irreflexive and the relation
\leq_{\mathcal{G}}
is antisymmetric.

{exa}

The ground-structure
\mathcal{G}
of the content of the box
o_{1}
of the PS
R
(the leftmost box of Figure
11
) is defined by:
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{G})=\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4}\}
,
\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{G})=\{p_{2}\}
,
l_{\mathcal{G}}(p_{1})=\bot=l_{\mathcal{G}}(p_{2})
,
l_{\mathcal{G}}(p_{3})=\wn
,
l_{\mathcal{G}}(p_{4})=1
,
t_{\mathcal{G}}(p_{2})=p_{3}
and
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{G})=\emptyset=\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{G})
.

Types will be used only in Subsection
4.2
. We introduce them right now in order to help the reader to see how ground-structures can represent MLL proofs.

We are given a set
\mathcal{X}
of propositional variables. We set
\mathcal{X}^{\perp}=\{X^{\perp};X\in\mathcal{X}\}
.
We define the set
\mathbb{T}
of
MELL types
as follows:
\mathbb{T}::=\mathcal{X}\>|\>\mathcal{X}^{\perp}\>|\>1\>|\>\bot|\>(\mathbb{T}\otimes\mathbb{T})\>|\>(\mathbb{T}\invamp\mathbb{T})\>|\>\oc\mathbb{T}\>|\>\wn\mathbb{T}
. We extend the operator
-^{\perp}
from the set
\mathcal{X}
to the set
\mathbb{T}
by defining
T^{\perp}\in\mathbb{T}
by induction on
T
, for any
T\in\mathbb{T}\setminus\mathcal{X}
, as follows:
(X^{\perp})^{\perp}=X
if
X\in\mathcal{X}
;
1^{\perp}=\bot
;
\bot^{\perp}=1
;
(A\otimes B)^{\perp}=(A^{\perp}\invamp B^{\perp})
;
(A\invamp B)^{\perp}=(A^{\perp}\otimes B^{\perp})
;
(\oc A)^{\perp}=\wn A^{\perp}
;
(\wn A)^{\perp}=\oc A^{\perp}
.

{defi}

A
typed simple differential net
(resp. a
typed ground-structure
) is a pair
(\mathcal{G},\mathsf{T})
such that
\mathcal{G}
is a pre-net (resp. a pre-ground-structure) and
\mathsf{T}
is a function
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{G})\to\mathbb{T}
such that

•

for any axiom
a
of
\mathcal{G}
, there exists a propositional variable
C
such that
\mathsf{T}[a]=\{C,C^{\perp}\}
;
14
14

14

Our typed proof-structures are
\eta
-expanded.

•

for any cut
c
of
\mathcal{G}
, there exists a MELL type
T
such that
\mathsf{T}[c]=\{T,T^{\perp}\}
;

and, for any
p\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{G})
, the following properties hold:

•

(l_{\mathcal{G}}(p)\in\{1,\bot\}\Rightarrow\mathsf{T}(p)=l_{\mathcal{G}}(p))
;

•

if
p\in\mathcal{P}^{\otimes}(\mathcal{G})
, then
\mathsf{T}(p)=(\mathsf{T}(w_{1})\otimes\mathsf{T}(w_{2}))
, where
w_{1}
(resp.
w_{2}
) is the left premise of
p
(resp. the right premise of
p
);

•

if
p\in\mathcal{P}^{\invamp}(\mathcal{G})
, then
\mathsf{T}(p)=(\mathsf{T}(w_{1})\invamp\mathsf{T}(w_{2}))
, where
w_{1}
(resp.
w_{2}
) is the left premise of
p
(resp. the right premise of
p
);

•

and, if
p
is an exponential port of
\mathcal{G}
, then there exists a MELL type
C
such that
(\mathsf{T}(p)=l_{\mathcal{G}}(p)C\wedge(\forall w\in\mathcal{W})(t_{\mathcal{G}}(w)=p\Rightarrow\mathsf{T}(w)=C))
.

Typing of exponential ports

Original cells of differential nets

Notice that the ports labelled by “
\oc
” are completely symmetric to the ports labelled by “
\wn
”: They can have any number of premises and the typing rule systematically introduces the connector
\oc
(see Figure
2
, while in
[
17
]
, there were three different kinds of
cells
: co-weakenings (of arity
0
) and co-derelictions (of arity
1
) that introduce the connector
\oc
, and co-contractions (of arity
2
) that do not modify the type (see Figure
2
).

Fact 1
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\mathsf{T})
be a typed ground-structure (resp. a typed simple differential net). Then
\mathcal{G}
is a ground-structure (resp. a simple differential net).

Proof 1.1
.

It is enough to notice that, for any
p\in\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{G})
, the size of
\mathsf{T}(t_{\mathcal{G}}(p))
is greater than the size of
\mathsf{T}(p)
.

A ground-structure
\mathcal{G}
such that
\mathcal{P}^{\oc}(\mathcal{G})=\emptyset
is essentially a PS of depth
0
, so MLL proofs can be represented by typed ground-structures.

\vdash B,B^{\perp}

\invamp

\vdash\underline{A},\underline{A}^{\perp}

\otimes

\vdash((A\otimes B)\otimes\underline{A}),(A^{\perp}\invamp B^{\perp}),\underline{A}^{\perp}

We can now define our notion of
PS
: We recall that this notion formalizes Danos & Regnier’s new syntax, and not Girard’s original syntax. Figure
10
, Figure
10
and Figure
10
illustrate some differences between the two syntaxes: Figure
10
and Figure
10
are two different objects in the original syntax, both of them are represented in the new syntax by the PS that is depicted in Figure
10
. In particular, in the new syntax, auxiliary doors of boxes are always premises of contractions. Since between auxiliary doors and contractions several box boundaries might be crossed, we need the auxiliary notion of
(differential) in-PS
. Concerning
differential PS’s
, it is worth noticing that the content of each of their boxes is an in-PS, in particular every
\oc
-port inside is always the main door of a box.

For any

of depth

in

d
(resp. an
in

d

such that

•

\mathcal{G}
is a simple differential net (resp. a ground-structure); we set
\mathcal{G}(S)=\mathcal{G}
;

•

\mathcal{B}_{0}\subseteq\{p\in\mathcal{P}^{\oc}(\mathcal{G});{\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{G}}(p)=0\}
(resp.
\mathcal{B}_{0}=\mathcal{P}^{\oc}(\mathcal{G})

\varepsilon\notin\mathcal{B}_{0}
and, for any pair
(p_{1},p_{2})\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{G})

p_{1}\notin\mathcal{B}_{0}
and, if
p_{1}
is a pair
(p,p^{\prime})
too, then
p\notin\mathcal{B}_{0}
;
16
16

16

We cannot simply disallow pairs in
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{G})
since in the definition of the differential in-PS
\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)
(Definition
2

the elements of
\mathcal{B}_{0}
are the
boxes of
S
at depth
0
;
17
17

17

We identify a box with its main door.

•

B_{0}
is a function that associates with every

an in-PS of depth
<d
that enjoys the following property: if

;

18

18

The function
B_{0}
maps boxes at depth
0
to their contents.
we set
\mathcal{P}(S)=\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{G})\cup\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}}(\{o\}\times\mathcal{P}(B_{0}(o)))
; the elements of
\mathcal{P}(S)
are the

S
;

•

t
is a partial function
\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}}(\{o\}\times\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(B_{0}(o)))\rightharpoonup\mathcal{P}^{\wn}(\mathcal{G})\cup\mathcal{B}_{0}

;

The function
t
maps to exponential ports at depth
0
their premises that are doors of boxes.

we set
\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(S)=\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{G}(S))\cup\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}}\{(o,q);(q\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(B_{0}(o))\wedge(o,q)\notin\mathsf{dom}(t))\}
and
\mathcal{P}_{>0}^{\mathsf{f}}(S)=\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(S)\setminus\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{G}(S))
; the elements of

(resp. of

We set
\mathcal{P}_{0}(S)=\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{G}(S))
(the elements of
\mathcal{P}_{0}(S)
are the
ports of
S
at depth
0
) and, for any

\mathcal{P}_{0}^{l}(S)=\mathcal{P}^{l}(\mathcal{G}(S))
. We set
\mathcal{W}_{0}(S)=\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{G}(S))
,
\mathcal{L}_{0}(S)=\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}(S))
,
\mathcal{A}_{0}(S)=\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{G}(S))

\mathcal{C}_{0}(S)=\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{G}(S))

is defined by setting
{\textit{a}}_{S}(p)={\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{G}(S)}(p)+\mathsf{Card}\left(\{q\in\mathsf{dom}(t);t(q)=p\}\right)
for any
p\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(S)
. The integer
\textit{co-size}(S)
is defined by induction on
\textit{depth}(S)
:
20
20

20

The supremum is taken in
\mathbb{N}
, hence, if
S

\textit{co-size}(S)=0
.

\textit{co-size}(S)=\sup{(\{{\textit{a}}_{S}(p);p\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(S)\}\cup\{\textit{co-size}(B_{0}(o));o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(S)\})}

We set
\mathcal{B}_{0}(S)=\mathcal{B}_{0}
,

and

. For any

\mathcal{P}_{S}^{\mathsf{f}}(o)=\{p\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(B_{S}(o));(o,p)\in\mathsf{dom}(t_{S})\}

21

21

Equivalently,
\mathcal{P}_{S}^{\mathsf{f}}(o)=\{p\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(B_{S}(o));(o,p)\notin\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(S)\}
.
We denote by

on
\mathbb{N}

and
\mathcal{P}_{\leq i}(S)=\mathcal{P}(S)\setminus\mathcal{P}_{>i}(S)
.

A
differential PS
(resp. a
PS
) is a differential in-PS (resp. an in-PS)
S
such that
\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(S)\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{G}(S))
.
22
22

22

Equivalently, a
differential PS
(resp. a
PS
) is a differential in-PS (resp. an in-PS)
S
such that
(\forall o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(S))\mathcal{P}_{S}^{\mathsf{f}}(o)=\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(B_{S}(o))
.

The set of
cocontractions
of an in-PS
S
is the set
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\oc}(S)\setminus\mathcal{B}_{0}(S)
. Notice that an in-PS is a differential in-PS with no co-contraction.

It is worth noticing that the binary relation
\leq_{S}
on the set
\mathcal{B}(S)\cup\{\varepsilon\}
defined by
((o_{1},\ldots,o_{m})\leq_{S}(o^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,o^{\prime}_{n})\Leftrightarrow(m\leq n\wedge(o_{1},\ldots,o_{m})=(o^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,o^{\prime}_{m})))
defines a tree with
\varepsilon
as the root.

In the absence of axioms and cuts, our definition of PS through in-PS’s is equivalent to our definition of PS in Definition 4 of
[
10
]
through
\circ
-PS’s. We removed
\circ
-ports because we simplified the proof of Proposition

and after this simplification they would not play any role any more (actually we introduced a syntactic construction

23

that, roughly speaking, can be seen as a partial recovery of these

{defi}

, for any

For any

, we define, by induction on

, the set of

•

(\mathcal{G}(S),\mathchoice{{\mathsf{T}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.55557pt,depth=3.02223pt}}_{\,\mathcal{P}_{0}(S)}}{{\mathsf{T}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.55557pt,depth=3.02223pt}}_{\,\mathcal{P}_{0}(S)}}{{\mathsf{T}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.88889pt,depth=2.41304pt}}_{\,\mathcal{P}_{0}(S)}}{{\mathsf{T}\,\smash{\vrule height=2.77777pt,depth=2.99861pt}}_{\,\mathcal{P}_{0}(S)}})

A
typed differential PS
(resp. a
typed PS
) is a typed differential in-PS (resp. a typed in-PS)
(S,\mathsf{T})
such that

1.2.
Isomorphisms

We want to consider PS’s up to the names of the ports, apart from the names of the shallow conclusions. We thus define the equivalence relation
\equiv
on PS’s; this relation is slightly finer than the equivalence relation
\simeq
, which ignores all the names of the ports.

{defi}

For any simple differential nets
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G^{\prime}}
, an isomorphism
\varphi
from
\mathcal{G}
to
\mathcal{G^{\prime}}
is a bijection
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{G})\simeq\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{G^{\prime}})
such that:

•

\varphi[\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{G})]=\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{G^{\prime}})

•

\varphi_{\ast}[\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{G})]=\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{G^{\prime}})

•

\varphi_{\ast}[\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{G})]=\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{G^{\prime}})

•

\varphi[\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G})]=\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G^{\prime}})

•

t_{\mathcal{G^{\prime}}}\circ\mathchoice{{\varphi\,\smash{\vrule height=3.44444pt,depth=3.02223pt}}_{\,\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{G})}}{{\varphi\,\smash{\vrule height=3.44444pt,depth=3.02223pt}}_{\,\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{G})}}{{\varphi\,\smash{\vrule height=2.41112pt,depth=2.20056pt}}_{\,\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{G})}}{{\varphi\,\smash{\vrule height=1.72221pt,depth=2.78612pt}}_{\,\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{G})}}=\varphi\circ t_{\mathcal{G}}

•

l_{\mathcal{G}}=l_{\mathcal{G^{\prime}}}\circ\varphi

We write
\varphi:\mathcal{G}\simeq\mathcal{G^{\prime}}
to denote that
\varphi
is an isomorphism from
\mathcal{G}
to
\mathcal{G^{\prime}}
; we write
\mathcal{G}\simeq\mathcal{G^{\prime}}
if there exists
\varphi
such that
\varphi:\mathcal{G}\simeq\mathcal{G^{\prime}}
.

Moreover, we write
\varphi:\mathcal{G}\equiv\mathcal{G^{\prime}}
to denote that
\varphi:\mathcal{G}\simeq\mathcal{G^{\prime}}
and
(\forall p\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{G}))\varphi(p)=p
; we write
\mathcal{G}\equiv\mathcal{G^{\prime}}
if there exists
\varphi
such that
\varphi:\mathcal{G}\equiv\mathcal{G^{\prime}}
.

{defi}

For any differential in-PS
S
of depth
d
, for any differential in-PS
S^{\prime}
, we define, by induction on
d
, the set of isomorphims from
S
to
S^{\prime}
: an isomorphism
\varphi
from
S
to
S^{\prime}
is a function
\mathcal{P}(S)\to\mathcal{P}(S^{\prime})
such that:

•

(\forall p\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(S))\varphi(p)\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(S^{\prime})
and the function
\mathcal{G}(\varphi):\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathcal{P}_{0}(S)&\to&\mathcal{P}_{0}(S^{\prime})\\
p&\mapsto&\varphi(p)\end{array}
is an isomorphism
\mathcal{G}(S)\simeq\mathcal{G}(S^{\prime})
;

•

\varphi[\mathcal{B}_{0}(S)]=\mathcal{B}_{0}(S^{\prime})
;

•

(\forall o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(S))(\forall p\in\mathcal{P}(B_{S}(o)))(\exists p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(B_{S^{\prime}}(\varphi(o))))\varphi(o,p)=(\varphi(o),p^{\prime})
and the function

\varphi_{o}:\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathcal{P}(B_{S}(o))&\to&\mathcal{P}(B_{S^{\prime}}(\varphi(o)))\\
p&\mapsto&\textit{$p^{\prime}$ such that $\varphi(o,p)=(\varphi(o),p^{\prime})$}\end{array}

is an isomorphism from
B_{S}(o)
to
B_{S^{\prime}}(\varphi(o))
;

•

\mathsf{dom}(t_{S^{\prime}})=\varphi[\mathsf{dom}(t_{S})]
and, for any
p\in\mathsf{dom}(t_{S})
, we have
(\varphi\circ t_{S})(p)=(t_{S^{\prime}}\circ\varphi)(p)
.

We write
\varphi:S\simeq S^{\prime}
to denote that
\varphi
is an isomorphism from
S
to
S^{\prime}
; we write
S\simeq S^{\prime}
if there exists
\varphi
such that
\varphi:S\simeq S^{\prime}
.

Moreover, we write
\varphi:S\equiv S^{\prime}
to denote that
\varphi:S\simeq S^{\prime}
and
(\forall p\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{G}(S)))\varphi(p)=p
; we write
S\equiv S^{\prime}
if there exists
\varphi
such that
\varphi:S\equiv S^{\prime}
.

Now, if
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{T^{\prime}}
are two sets of differential in-PS’s, we write
\mathcal{T}\equiv\mathcal{T^{\prime}}
if there exists a bijection
\varphi:\mathcal{T}\simeq\mathcal{T^{\prime}}
such that, for any
T\in\mathcal{T}
, we have
T\equiv\varphi(T)
.

Finally, if
(S,\mathsf{T})
and
(S^{\prime},\mathsf{T^{\prime}})
are two typed differential in-PS’s, then we write
(S,\mathsf{T})\equiv(S^{\prime},\mathsf{T^{\prime}})
if there exists
\varphi:S\equiv S^{\prime}
such that
\mathsf{T}=\mathsf{T^{\prime}}\circ\varphi
.

Fact 2
.

Let
(S,\mathsf{T})
and
(S^{\prime},\mathsf{T^{\prime}})
be two cut-free typed differential in-PS’s such that
S\equiv S^{\prime}
. If
\mathchoice{{\mathsf{T}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.55557pt,depth=4.07626pt}}_{\,\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(S)}}{{\mathsf{T}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.55557pt,depth=4.07626pt}}_{\,\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(S)}}{{\mathsf{T}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.88889pt,depth=3.19223pt}}_{\,\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(S)}}{{\mathsf{T}\,\smash{\vrule height=2.77777pt,depth=3.77779pt}}_{\,\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(S)}}=\mathchoice{{\mathsf{T^{\prime}}\,\smash{\vrule height=6.41428pt,depth=4.07626pt}}_{\,\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(S^{\prime})}}{{\mathsf{T^{\prime}}\,\smash{\vrule height=6.01427pt,depth=4.07626pt}}_{\,\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(S^{\prime})}}{{\mathsf{T^{\prime}}\,\smash{\vrule height=4.63649pt,depth=3.19223pt}}_{\,\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(S^{\prime})}}{{\mathsf{T^{\prime}}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.37779pt,depth=3.77779pt}}_{\,\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(S^{\prime})}}
, then
(S,\mathsf{T})\equiv(S^{\prime},\mathsf{T^{\prime}})
.

Another variant of the notion of isomorphism will be defined in the next subsection (Definition
1.3
). A special case of isomorphism consists in renaming only ports at depth
0
:

{defi}

Let
S
and
S^{\prime}
be two differential in-PS’s. Let
\varphi
be a bijection
\mathcal{P}\simeq\mathcal{P^{\prime}}
, where
\mathcal{P^{\prime}}\cap(\mathcal{P}_{0}(S)\setminus\mathcal{P})=\emptyset
. We say that
S^{\prime}
is obtained from
S
by renaming the ports via
\varphi
and we write
S^{\prime}=S[\varphi]
if the following properties hold:

•

\mathcal{P}_{0}(S^{\prime})=\overline{\varphi}[\mathcal{P}_{0}(S)]

•

\mathcal{W}_{0}(S^{\prime})=\overline{\varphi}[\mathcal{W}_{0}(S)]

•

\mathcal{A}_{0}(S^{\prime})=\{\overline{\varphi}[a];a\in\mathcal{A}_{0}(S)\}

•

\mathcal{C}_{0}(S^{\prime})=\{\overline{\varphi}[a];a\in\mathcal{C}_{0}(S)\}

•

\mathcal{L}_{0}(S^{\prime})=\overline{\varphi}[\mathcal{L}_{0}(S)]

•

l_{\mathcal{G}(S^{\prime})}\circ\overline{\varphi}=l_{\mathcal{G}(S)}

•

t_{\mathcal{G}(S^{\prime})}\circ\overline{\varphi}=\overline{\varphi}\circ t_{\mathcal{G}(S)}

•

\mathcal{B}_{0}(S^{\prime})=\overline{\varphi}[\mathcal{B}_{0}(S)]

•

\mathsf{dom}(t_{S^{\prime}})=\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(S)}\{(\overline{\varphi}(o),p);p\in\mathcal{P}_{S}^{\mathsf{f}}(o)\}
and, for any
(o,p)\in\mathsf{dom}(t_{S})
, we have
t_{S^{\prime}}(\overline{\varphi}(o),p)=t_{S}(o,p)

•

and
B_{S^{\prime}}=B_{S}\circ\overline{\varphi}
,

where
\overline{\varphi}
is the function
\mathcal{P}_{0}(S)\to\mathcal{P}_{0}(S^{\prime})
that associates with every
p\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(S)
the following port of
\mathcal{G}(S^{\prime})
:
\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}p&\textit{if $p\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(S)\setminus\mathcal{P}$;}\\
\varphi(p)&\textit{if $p\in\mathcal{P}$.}\end{array}\right.

If
S^{\prime}=S[\varphi]
and
\varphi
is, for some singleton
\mathcal{E}=\{a\}
, the bijection
\mathcal{P}_{0}(S)\simeq\mathcal{E}\times\mathcal{P}_{0}(S)
that associates with every
p\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(S)
the pair
(a,p)
, then
S^{\prime}
is denoted by
\langle a,S\rangle
too.

1.3.
Some operations on differential proof-structures

In this subsection, we describe some operations to obtain new PS’s from old ones.

For any differential in-PS
S
, for any integer
i
, we define a differential in-PS
S^{\leq i}
of depth
\leq i
, which is obtained from
S
by removing some boxes:

{defi}

Let
S
be a differential in-PS and let
i\in\mathbb{N}
. We denote by
S^{\leq i}
the differential in-PS such that
\mathcal{G}(S^{\leq i})=\mathcal{G}(S)
,
\mathcal{B}_{0}(S^{\leq i})=\mathcal{B}_{0}^{<i}(S)
,
B_{S^{\leq i}}=\mathchoice{{B_{S}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=3.95737pt}}_{\,\mathcal{B}_{0}^{<i}(S)}}{{B_{S}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=3.95737pt}}_{\,\mathcal{B}_{0}^{<i}(S)}}{{B_{S}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.82668pt,depth=3.28581pt}}_{\,\mathcal{B}_{0}^{<i}(S)}}{{B_{S}\,\smash{\vrule height=2.73334pt,depth=3.87137pt}}_{\,\mathcal{B}_{0}^{<i}(S)}}
and
t_{S^{\leq i}}=\mathchoice{{t_{S}\,\smash{\vrule height=4.92064pt,depth=9.39735pt}}_{\,\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{<i}(S)}(\{o\}\times\mathcal{P}_{S}^{\mathsf{f}}(o))}}{{t_{S}\,\smash{\vrule height=4.92064pt,depth=9.39735pt}}_{\,\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{<i}(S)}(\{o\}\times\mathcal{P}_{S}^{\mathsf{f}}(o))}}{{t_{S}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.44446pt,depth=8.57565pt}}_{\,\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{<i}(S)}(\{o\}\times\mathcal{P}_{S}^{\mathsf{f}}(o))}}{{t_{S}\,\smash{\vrule height=2.46031pt,depth=9.16121pt}}_{\,\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{<i}(S)}(\{o\}\times\mathcal{P}_{S}^{\mathsf{f}}(o))}}
.

In particular
S^{\leq 0}
is essentially the same object as
\mathcal{G}(S)
.

Remark 3
.

If
\textit{depth}(T)<i
, then
T^{\leq i}=T
.

Remark 4
.

We have
{(S^{\leq i})}^{\leq i^{\prime}}=S^{\leq\min\{i,i^{\prime}\}}
.

{exa}

The differential PS
R^{\leq 1}
, where
R
is the PS depicted in Figure
11
, is depicted in Figure
35
, p.
35
.

We can also erase some ports at depth
0
:

{defi}

Let
S^{\prime}
and
S
be two differential in-PS’s. Let
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{0}(S)
. We write
S^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{Q}}S
to denote that
\mathcal{P}_{0}(S^{\prime})\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{0}(S)
,
\mathcal{W}_{0}(S^{\prime})=\{w\in(\mathcal{W}_{0}(S)\cap\mathcal{P}_{0}(S^{\prime}))\setminus(\mathcal{Q}\cap\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\textit{e}}(S));t_{\mathcal{G}(S)}(w)\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(S^{\prime})\}
,
l_{\mathcal{G}(S^{\prime})}=\mathchoice{{l_{\mathcal{G}(S)}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.55557pt,depth=3.486pt}}_{\,\mathcal{P}_{0}(S^{\prime})}}{{l_{\mathcal{G}(S)}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.55557pt,depth=3.486pt}}_{\,\mathcal{P}_{0}(S^{\prime})}}{{l_{\mathcal{G}(S)}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.88889pt,depth=2.81444pt}}_{\,\mathcal{P}_{0}(S^{\prime})}}{{l_{\mathcal{G}(S)}\,\smash{\vrule height=2.77777pt,depth=3.40001pt}}_{\,\mathcal{P}_{0}(S^{\prime})}}
,
t_{\mathcal{G}(S^{\prime})}=\mathchoice{{t_{\mathcal{G}(S)}\,\smash{\vrule height=4.92064pt,depth=3.486pt}}_{\,\mathcal{W}_{0}(S^{\prime})}}{{t_{\mathcal{G}(S)}\,\smash{\vrule height=4.92064pt,depth=3.486pt}}_{\,\mathcal{W}_{0}(S^{\prime})}}{{t_{\mathcal{G}(S)}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.44446pt,depth=2.81444pt}}_{\,\mathcal{W}_{0}(S^{\prime})}}{{t_{\mathcal{G}(S)}\,\smash{\vrule height=2.46031pt,depth=3.40001pt}}_{\,\mathcal{W}_{0}(S^{\prime})}}
,
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}(S^{\prime}))=\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}(S))\cap\{w\in\mathcal{W}_{0}(S);t_{\mathcal{G}(S)}(w)\in\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\textit{m}}(S^{\prime})\}
,
\mathcal{A}_{0}(S^{\prime})=\{a\in\mathcal{A}_{0}(S);\bigcup a\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{0}(S^{\prime})\}
,
\mathcal{C}_{0}(S^{\prime})=\{a\in\mathcal{C}_{0}(S);\bigcup a\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{0}(S^{\prime})\setminus(\mathcal{Q}\cap\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\textit{e}}(S))\}
,
\mathcal{B}_{0}(S^{\prime})=\mathcal{B}_{0}(S)\cap\mathcal{P}_{0}(S^{\prime})
,
B_{S^{\prime}}=\mathchoice{{B_{S}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=3.486pt}}_{\,\mathcal{B}_{0}(S^{\prime})}}{{B_{S}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=3.486pt}}_{\,\mathcal{B}_{0}(S^{\prime})}}{{B_{S}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.82668pt,depth=2.81444pt}}_{\,\mathcal{B}_{0}(S^{\prime})}}{{B_{S}\,\smash{\vrule height=2.73334pt,depth=3.40001pt}}_{\,\mathcal{B}_{0}(S^{\prime})}}
and
t_{S^{\prime}}=\mathchoice{{t_{S}\,\smash{\vrule height=4.92064pt,depth=9.39735pt}}_{\,\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(S^{\prime})}(\{o\}\times\mathcal{P}_{S}^{\mathsf{f}}(o))}}{{t_{S}\,\smash{\vrule height=4.92064pt,depth=9.39735pt}}_{\,\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(S^{\prime})}(\{o\}\times\mathcal{P}_{S}^{\mathsf{f}}(o))}}{{t_{S}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.44446pt,depth=8.57565pt}}_{\,\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(S^{\prime})}(\{o\}\times\mathcal{P}_{S}^{\mathsf{f}}(o))}}{{t_{S}\,\smash{\vrule height=2.46031pt,depth=9.16121pt}}_{\,\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(S^{\prime})}(\{o\}\times\mathcal{P}_{S}^{\mathsf{f}}(o))}}
.

We write
S^{\prime}\sqsubseteq S
if there exists
\mathcal{Q}
such that
S^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{Q}}S
.

Remark 5
.

We have
S^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{Q}}S
if and only if
S^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{Q}\cap\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\textit{e}}(S)}S
.

Remark 6
.

If
S^{\prime}\sqsubseteq S
, then
\mathcal{P}_{0}(S^{\prime})\cap\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{G}(S))\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{G}(S^{\prime}))
.

Remark 7
.

If
S^{\prime},S^{\prime\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{Q}}S
and
\mathcal{P}_{0}(S^{\prime})=\mathcal{P}_{0}(S^{\prime\prime})
, then
S^{\prime}=S^{\prime\prime}
. So, if, for some
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{0}(S)
, there exists a differential in-PS
S^{\prime}
such that
\mathcal{P}_{0}(S^{\prime})=\mathcal{P}
and
S^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\emptyset}S
, then we can denote by
\mathchoice{{S\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=1.27501pt}}_{\,\mathcal{P}}}{{S\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=1.27501pt}}_{\,\mathcal{P}}}{{S\,\smash{\vrule height=3.82668pt,depth=0.85pt}}_{\,\mathcal{P}}}{{S\,\smash{\vrule height=2.73334pt,depth=1.4251pt}}_{\,\mathcal{P}}}
the unique such differential in-PS
S^{\prime}
.

Remark 8
.

We have
S^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{Q}}S
if, and only if, the following properties hold:

•

{S^{\prime}}^{\leq 0}\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{Q}}S^{\leq 0}

•

\mathcal{B}_{0}(S^{\prime})=\mathcal{B}_{0}(S)\cap\mathcal{P}_{0}(S^{\prime})

•

B_{S^{\prime}}=\mathchoice{{B_{S}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=3.486pt}}_{\,\mathcal{B}_{0}(S^{\prime})}}{{B_{S}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=3.486pt}}_{\,\mathcal{B}_{0}(S^{\prime})}}{{B_{S}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.82668pt,depth=2.81444pt}}_{\,\mathcal{B}_{0}(S^{\prime})}}{{B_{S}\,\smash{\vrule height=2.73334pt,depth=3.40001pt}}_{\,\mathcal{B}_{0}(S^{\prime})}}

•

t_{S^{\prime}}=\mathchoice{{t_{S}\,\smash{\vrule height=4.92064pt,depth=9.39735pt}}_{\,\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(S^{\prime})}(\{o\}\times\mathcal{P}_{S}^{\mathsf{f}}(o))}}{{t_{S}\,\smash{\vrule height=4.92064pt,depth=9.39735pt}}_{\,\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(S^{\prime})}(\{o\}\times\mathcal{P}_{S}^{\mathsf{f}}(o))}}{{t_{S}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.44446pt,depth=8.57565pt}}_{\,\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(S^{\prime})}(\{o\}\times\mathcal{P}_{S}^{\mathsf{f}}(o))}}{{t_{S}\,\smash{\vrule height=2.46031pt,depth=9.16121pt}}_{\,\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(S^{\prime})}(\{o\}\times\mathcal{P}_{S}^{\mathsf{f}}(o))}}

Remark 9
.

If
S^{\prime}_{1}\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{Q}}S_{1}
and
\varphi:S_{1}\simeq S_{2}
, then there exists a unique
S^{\prime}_{2}\sqsubseteq_{\varphi[\mathcal{Q}]}S_{2}
such that there exists an isomorphism
S^{\prime}_{1}\simeq S^{\prime}_{2}
associating with every port
p
of
S^{\prime}_{1}
the port
\varphi(p)
of
S_{2}
.

Fact 10
.

Let
S
,
S^{\prime}
and
S^{\prime\prime}
be three differential in-PS’s. Let
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{0}(S)
and
\mathcal{Q^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{0}(S^{\prime})
. If
S^{\prime\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{Q^{\prime}}}S^{\prime}
and
S^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{Q}}S
, then
S^{\prime\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{Q}\cup\mathcal{Q^{\prime}}}S
.

Fact 11
.

Let
S^{\prime}
and
S
be two differential in-PS’s and let
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\textit{e}}(S)
such that
S^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{Q}}S
. Let
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
S^{\prime\leq i}\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{Q}}S^{\leq i}
.

The operator
\bigoplus
glues together several differential in-PS’s that share only shallow conclusions that are contractions:
24
24

24

This operation has nothing to do with the additive
\oplus
of linear logic, it is rather essentially the mix of linear logic.

{defi}

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a finite set of differential in-PS’s. We say that
\mathcal{U}
is
gluable
if, for any
T,T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
such that
T\not=T^{\prime}
, we have
\mathcal{P}_{0}(T)\cap\mathcal{P}_{0}(T^{\prime})\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{G}(T))\cap\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\wn}(T)\cap\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{G}(T^{\prime}))\cap\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\wn}(T^{\prime})
and, for any pair
(p_{1},p_{2})\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(T)
, we have
p_{1}\notin\mathcal{B}_{0}(T^{\prime})
and, if
p_{1}
is a pair
(p,p^{\prime})
too, then
p\notin\mathcal{B}_{0}(T^{\prime})
.

If
\mathcal{U}
is gluable, then
\bigoplus\mathcal{U}
is the differential in-PS such that:

•

\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bigoplus\mathcal{U})=\bigcup_{T\in\mathcal{U}}\mathcal{P}_{0}(T)

•

\mathcal{W}_{0}(\bigoplus\mathcal{U})=\bigcup_{T\in\mathcal{U}}\mathcal{W}_{0}(T)

•

l_{\mathcal{G}(\bigoplus\mathcal{U})}(p)=l_{\mathcal{G}(T)}(p)
for any
p\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(\bigoplus\mathcal{U})
and any
T\in\mathcal{U}
such that
p\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(T)

•

\mathcal{A}_{0}(\bigoplus\mathcal{U})=\bigcup_{T\in\mathcal{U}}\mathcal{A}_{0}(T)

•

\mathcal{C}_{0}(\bigoplus\mathcal{U})=\bigcup_{T\in\mathcal{U}}\mathcal{C}_{0}(T)

•

\mathcal{L}_{0}(\bigoplus\mathcal{U})=\bigcup_{T\in\mathcal{U}}\mathcal{L}_{0}(T)

•

t_{\mathcal{G}(\bigoplus\mathcal{U})}(p)=t_{\mathcal{G}(T)}(p)
for any
p\in\mathcal{W}_{0}(\bigoplus\mathcal{U})
and any
T\in\mathcal{U}
such that
p\in\mathcal{W}_{0}(T)
;

•

\mathcal{B}_{0}(\bigoplus\mathcal{U})(p)=\bigcup_{T\in\mathcal{U}}\mathcal{B}_{0}(T)

•

B_{\bigoplus\mathcal{U}}(o)=B_{T}(o)
for any
o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(\bigoplus\mathcal{U})
and any
T\in\mathcal{U}
such that
o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(T)
;

•

\mathsf{dom}(t_{\bigoplus\mathcal{U}})=\bigcup_{T\in\mathcal{U}}\mathsf{dom}(t_{T})
and
t_{\bigoplus\mathcal{U}}(p)=t_{T}(p)
for any
p\in\mathsf{dom}(t_{\bigoplus\mathcal{U}})
and any
T\in\mathcal{U}
such that
p\in\mathsf{dom}(t_{T})
.

Remark 12
.

If
\mathcal{U}
is gluable, then
(\bigoplus\mathcal{U})^{\leq i}=\bigoplus\{U^{\leq i};U\in\mathcal{U}\}
.

We can add wires:

{defi}

Let
S
be a differential in-PS. Let
\mathcal{W}\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(S)
and
\mathcal{W^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\textit{e}}(S)\setminus\mathcal{B}_{0}(S)
such that
(\forall p\in\mathcal{W}\cap\mathcal{P}_{0}(S))(\forall p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W^{\prime}})\neg p\leq_{\mathcal{G}(S)}p^{\prime}
. Let
t
be a function
\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{W^{\prime}}
. Then we denote by
S@t
the differential in-PS such that

•

\mathfrak{t}_{S@t}
is the extension of
\mathfrak{t}_{S}
such that
\mathsf{dom}(\mathfrak{t}_{S@t})=\mathsf{dom}(\mathfrak{t}_{S})\cup\mathcal{W}
and
(\forall p\in\mathcal{W})\mathfrak{t}_{S@t}(p)=t(p)

•

and
\mathcal{P}_{0}(S@t)=\mathcal{P}_{0}(S)
,
l_{\mathcal{G}(S@t)}=l_{\mathcal{G}(S)}
,
\mathcal{L}_{0}(S@t)=\mathcal{L}_{0}(S)
,
\mathcal{A}_{0}(S@t)=\mathcal{A}_{0}(S)
,
\mathcal{C}_{0}(S@t)=\mathcal{C}_{0}(S)
,
\mathcal{B}_{0}(S@t)=\mathcal{B}_{0}(S)
,
B_{S@t}=B_{S}
.

Remark 13
.

We have
(S@t)^{\leq i}=(S^{\leq i})@\mathchoice{{t\,\smash{\vrule height=4.92064pt,depth=3.47449pt}}_{\,\mathcal{P}_{\leq i}(S)}}{{t\,\smash{\vrule height=4.92064pt,depth=3.47449pt}}_{\,\mathcal{P}_{\leq i}(S)}}{{t\,\smash{\vrule height=3.44446pt,depth=3.66338pt}}_{\,\mathcal{P}_{\leq i}(S)}}{{t\,\smash{\vrule height=2.46031pt,depth=4.24893pt}}_{\,\mathcal{P}_{\leq i}(S)}}
.

We can remove shallow conclusions:

{defi}

Let
T
be a differential in-PS such that
\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{G}(T))\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\textit{e}}(T)\setminus\mathcal{B}_{0}(T)
. Then
\overline{T}
is the unique differential in-PS such that

•

\mathcal{P}_{0}(\overline{T})=\mathcal{P}_{0}(T)\setminus\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{G}(T))

•

and
T=(\overline{T}\oplus\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{G}(T)))@t
, where
t
is the function that associates with every
p\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathfrak{t}_{T})
such that
\mathfrak{t}_{T}(p)\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{G}(T))
the port
\mathfrak{t}_{T}(p)
.

If
\mathcal{T}
is a set of differential in-PS’s, then
\overline{\mathcal{T}}=\{\overline{T};T\in\mathcal{T}\}
.

This operation allows to define the following variant of the notion of isomorphism of differential in-PS’s:

{defi}

Let
S
and
U
be two differential in-PS’s. Let
o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(S)
such that
\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(U)\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{S}^{\mathsf{f}}(o)
. Let
T
be a differential in-PS such that
\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{G}(T))\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\textit{e}}(T)\setminus\mathcal{B}_{0}(T)
. Then we write
\varphi:U\equiv_{(S,o)}T
if
\varphi:U\simeq\overline{T}
such that, for any
p\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(U)
, we have
t_{S}(o,p)=\mathfrak{t}_{T}(\varphi(p))
. We write
U\equiv_{(S,o)}T
if there exists
\varphi
such that
\varphi:U\equiv_{(S,o)}T
.

Remark 14
.

Notice that:

•

While the relations
\simeq
and
\equiv
are symmetric, the relation
\equiv_{(S,o)}
is
not
symmetric.

•

If
\varphi:U\equiv_{(S,o)}T
and
\psi:U^{\prime}\equiv U
, then
\varphi\circ\psi:U^{\prime}\equiv_{(S,o)}T
.

•

If
\varphi_{1}:U\equiv_{(S,o)}T_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}:U\equiv_{(S,o)}T_{2}
, then
\psi:T_{1}\equiv T_{2}
, where
\psi
is the bijection
\mathcal{P}(T_{1})\simeq\mathcal{P}(T_{2})
defined by
\psi(p)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\varphi_{2}({\varphi_{1}}^{-1}(p))&\textit{if $p\notin\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{G}(T_{1}))$;}\\
p&\textit{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

The following operation consists in adding contractions as shallow conclusions to the content of some box
o
; the conclusions of the box
o
that were contracted at depth
0
are now contracted inside the box
o
. We can then define a complexity measure on in-PS’s that decreases with this operation, which allows to prove Proposition
45
by induction on this complexity measure.

{defi}

Let
R
and
R_{o}
be two in-PS’s. Let
o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(R)\cap\mathcal{B}_{0}(R_{o})
. Let
\mathcal{Q^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\wn}(B_{R_{o}}(o))
and let
\varphi
be a bijection
\mathcal{P}_{R}^{\wn}(o)\simeq\mathcal{Q^{\prime}}
. We say that
R_{o}
is obtained from
R
by adding, according to
\varphi
, contractions as shallow conclusions to the content of the box
o
and we write
R_{o}=\varphi\cdot_{o}R
if the following properties hold:

•

{R_{o}}^{\leq 0}=R^{\leq 0}
;

•

\mathcal{B}_{0}(R_{o})=\mathcal{B}_{0}(R)
;

•

B_{R_{o}}(o^{\prime})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}B_{R}(o^{\prime})&\textit{if $o^{\prime}\not=o$;}\\
R^{\prime}_{o}&\textit{if $o^{\prime}=o$;}\end{array}\right.
with
{R^{\prime}_{o}}=({B_{R}(o)}\oplus\bigoplus_{q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q^{\prime}}}\wn_{q^{\prime}})@t
, where
\mathcal{Q^{\prime}}
is a disjoint set from
\mathcal{P}_{0}(B_{R}(o))
and
t
is the function
\mathcal{P}_{R}^{\mathsf{f}}(o)\setminus\{{\oc}_{R}(o)\}\to\mathcal{Q^{\prime}}
that associates with every
p\in\mathcal{P}_{R}^{\mathsf{f}}(o)\setminus\{{\oc}_{R}(o)\}
the port
\varphi(t_{R}(o,p))
;

•

and
t_{R_{o}}=\mathchoice{{t_{R}\,\smash{\vrule height=4.92064pt,depth=5.57225pt}}_{\,\mathsf{dom}(t_{R})\setminus(\{o\}\times(\mathcal{P}_{R}^{\mathsf{f}}(o)\setminus\{{\oc}_{R}(o)\}))}}{{t_{R}\,\smash{\vrule height=4.92064pt,depth=5.57225pt}}_{\,\mathsf{dom}(t_{R})\setminus(\{o\}\times(\mathcal{P}_{R}^{\mathsf{f}}(o)\setminus\{{\oc}_{R}(o)\}))}}{{t_{R}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.44446pt,depth=6.45059pt}}_{\,\mathsf{dom}(t_{R})\setminus(\{o\}\times(\mathcal{P}_{R}^{\mathsf{f}}(o)\setminus\{{\oc}_{R}(o)\}))}}{{t_{R}\,\smash{\vrule height=2.46031pt,depth=7.03615pt}}_{\,\mathsf{dom}(t_{R})\setminus(\{o\}\times(\mathcal{P}_{R}^{\mathsf{f}}(o)\setminus\{{\oc}_{R}(o)\}))}}
.

Remark 15
.

For any
o^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(B_{R}(o))
, we have

•

\mathcal{P}_{{B_{R}(o)}}^{\mathsf{f}}(o^{\prime})=

\{p\in\mathcal{P}_{{B_{(\varphi\cdot_{o}R)}(o)}}^{\mathsf{f}}(o^{\prime});

t_{B_{(\varphi\cdot_{o}R)}(o)}(o^{\prime},p)\notin\mathcal{Q^{\prime}}\}

•

and
(\forall p\in\mathcal{P}_{{B_{R}(o)}}^{\mathsf{f}}(o^{\prime}))t_{B_{R}(o)}(o^{\prime},p)=t_{B_{(\varphi\cdot_{o}R)}(o)}(o^{\prime},p)
.

Moreover, for any
q\in\mathcal{P}_{R}^{\mathsf{f}}(o)
such that
t_{B_{(\varphi\cdot_{o}R)}(o)}(q)\in\mathcal{Q^{\prime}}
, we have
t_{B_{(\varphi\cdot_{o}R)}(o)}(q)=\varphi(t_{R}(o,q))
.

Notice that, if
R
is a PS, then
B_{(\varphi\cdot_{o}R)}(o)
is a PS too (while
B_{R}(o)
is not necessarily a PS); we will implicitly use this property in the proofs of Lemma
42
and Proposition
48
.

{exa}

The in-PS
\varphi\cdot_{o_{3}}R
, where
\varphi
is some bijection
\mathcal{P}_{R}^{\wn}(o_{3})\simeq\mathcal{Q^{\prime}}
, is depicted in Figure
12
.

2.
Taylor expansion

When Jean-Yves Girard introduced proof-nets in
[
19
]
, he also introduced
experiments on proof-nets
. Experiments (see our Definition
4.2
in Section
4
) are a technology allowing to compute pointwisely the interpretation
\llbracket R\rrbracket
of a proof-net
R
in the model directly on the proof-net rather than through some sequent calculus proof obtained from one of its sequentializations: the set of
results
of all the experiments on a given proof-net is its interpretation
\llbracket R\rrbracket
. In an untyped framework, experiments correspond to derivations of intersection types and results correspond to intersection types.

Inspired by this notion, we introduce
pseudo-experiments
.

{defi}

For any differential in-PS
R
, we define, by induction on
\textit{depth}(R)
, the set
\mathfrak{E}(R)
of
pseudo-experiments on
R
: it is the set of functions that associate with every
o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(R)
a finite set of pseudo-experiments on
B_{R}(o)
and with
\varepsilon
some
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

Given a pseudo-experiment
e
on a differential in-PS
R
, we define, by induction on
\textit{depth}(R)
, the function
e^{\#}:\mathcal{B}(R)\rightarrow\mathfrak{P}_{\textit{fin}}(\mathbb{N})
as follows: for any
o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(R)
,
e^{\#}(o)=\{\mathsf{Card}\left(e(o)\right)\}
and, for any
o^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}(B_{R}(o))
,
e^{\#}(o,o^{\prime})=\bigcup_{e_{o}\in e(o)}{e_{o}}^{\#}(o^{\prime})
.

Our definition is quite
ad hoc
; actually, what we have in mind is the following notion of
canonical pseudo-experiment
: A canonical pseudo-experiment on a differential in-PS
R
is a function that associates with every
o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(R)
a finite multiset of canonical pseudo-experiments on
B_{R}(o)
. Thus a canonical pseudo-experiment is an experiment without any labels on the axioms and any constraints on cuts. If we consider pseudo-experiments instead of canonical pseudo-experiments, it is only in order to be less verbose. For instance, if we used canonical pseudo-experiments instead of pseudo-experiments, the differential in-PS
S
of Definition
2
could be defined by setting

S=R^{\leq i}\oplus\bigoplus_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)}\bigoplus_{e_{o}\in\textit{Supp}(e(o))}\bigoplus_{z\in\{1,\ldots,e(e_{o})\}}\langle o,\langle(e_{o},z),\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})\rangle\rangle

instead of
S=R^{\leq i}\oplus\bigoplus_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)}\bigoplus_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\langle o,\langle e_{o},\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})\rangle\rangle
.

{exa}

There exists a pseudo-experiment
e
on the PS
R
of Figure
11
such that
e^{\#}(o_{1})=\{10^{223}\}
,
e^{\#}(o_{2})=\{10\}
,
e^{\#}(o_{3})=\{10^{224}\}
,
e^{\#}(o_{4})=\{100\}
,
e^{\#}((o_{2},o))

=

\{10^{3},\ldots,10^{12}\}
,
e^{\#}((o_{2},o^{\prime}))

=

\{10^{13},\ldots,10^{22}\}
,
e^{\#}((o_{4},o))

=

\{10^{23},\ldots,10^{122}\}
and
e^{\#}((o_{4},o^{\prime}))

=

\{10^{123},\ldots,10^{222}\}
.

For defining Taylor expansion, we only need to define
\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)
with
i=0
, which is the differential in-PS obtained by (fully) expanding the boxes according to
e
. But a key tool of the proof is the introduction of the differential in-PS’s
\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)
with
i>0
, which are the differential in-PS’s obtained by expanding only the boxes of depth at least
i
. We recall that the notation
\langle o,R\rangle
for any differential in-PS
R
was introduced in Definition
1.2
.

{defi}

Let
R
be an in-PS of depth
d
. Let
e
be a pseudo-experiment on
R
. Let
i\in\mathbb{N}
.
We define, by induction on
d
, a differential in-PS
\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)
of depth
\min\{i,d\}
and a function
\kappa_{R}[i](e):\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e))\to\mathcal{P}(R)
as follows: We set
\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)=S@t
, where

S=R^{\leq i}\oplus\bigoplus_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)}\bigoplus_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\langle o,\langle e_{o},\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})\rangle\rangle

and
t
is the function
\mathcal{W}_{0}\cup\mathcal{W}_{>0}\to\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\textit{e}}(R)
with

•

\mathcal{W}_{0}=\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)}\bigcup_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\{(o,(e_{o},q));(q\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})))\wedge\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(q)\in\mathcal{P}_{R}^{\mathsf{f}}(o))\}

•

\mathcal{W}_{>0}=\bigcup_{(o,(e_{o},o^{\prime}))\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(S)\setminus\mathcal{B}_{0}(R^{\leq i})}\left\{((o,(e_{o},o^{\prime})),q);\begin{array}[]{ll}((o^{\prime},q)\in\mathcal{P}_{>0}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o}))\\
\wedge\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(o^{\prime},q)\in\mathcal{P}_{R}^{\mathsf{f}}(o))\end{array}\right\}

•

and
t(p)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}t_{R}(o,\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(q))&\textit{if $p=(o,(e_{o},q))\in\mathcal{W}_{0}$;}\\
t_{R}(o,\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(o^{\prime},q))&\textit{if $p=((o,(e_{o},o^{\prime})),q)\in\mathcal{W}_{>0}$.}\end{array}\right.

For any
p\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e))
, the port
\kappa_{R}[i](e)(p)
of
R
is the following one:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}p&\textit{if $p\in\mathcal{P}(R^{\leq i})$;}\\
(o,\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(p^{\prime}))&\textit{if $p=(o,(e_{o},p^{\prime}))\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e))\setminus\mathcal{P}_{0}(R)$;}\\
(o,\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(o^{\prime},p^{\prime}))&\textit{if $p=((o,(e_{o},o^{\prime})),p^{\prime})$ and $(o,(e_{o},o^{\prime}))\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e))\setminus\mathcal{B}_{0}(R^{\leq i})$.}\end{array}\right.

If
o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)
and
e_{o}
is a pseudo-experiment on
B_{R}(o)
, then we set

\displaystyle R\langle o,i,e_{o}\rangle=\langle o,\langle e_{o},\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})\rangle\rangle.

Remark 16
.

We have
\mathcal{W}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{W}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e))
and
\mathsf{dom}(t_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)})
is the set

\mathsf{dom}(t_{R^{\leq i}})\cup\left(\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)}\bigcup_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\bigcup_{o^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o}))}\{((o,(e_{o},o^{\prime})),p);p\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})}^{\mathsf{f}}(o^{\prime})\}\right)\cup\mathcal{W}_{>0}

Moreover, for any
p\in\mathsf{dom}(t_{R^{\leq i}})
, we have
t_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)}(p)=t_{R}(p)
.

Notice that if
R
is cut-free, then
\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)
is cut-free too. The conclusions are the duplications of the conclusions; in particular, if
R
is a PS, then
\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)
is a differential PS:

Fact 17
.

Let
R
be an in-PS. Let
e
be a pseudo-experiment on
R
. Let
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Then we have
(\forall q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)))(\kappa_{R}[i](e)(q)\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(R)\Leftrightarrow q\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)))
.

Proof 2.1
.

By induction on
\textit{depth}(R)
. Let
q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e))
such that
\kappa_{R}[i](e)(q)\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(R)
. We distinguish between two cases:

•

\kappa_{R}[i](e)(q)\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(R^{\leq i})
: we have
q=\kappa_{R}[i](e)(q)\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(R^{\leq i})\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e))
;

•

There exist
o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)
and
p\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(B_{R}(o))\setminus\mathcal{P}_{R}^{\mathsf{f}}(o)
such that
\kappa_{R}[i](e)(q)=(o,p)
: Then

–

either there exist
e_{o}\in e(o)
and
q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o}))
such that
q=(o,(e_{o},q^{\prime}))
and
\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(q^{\prime})=p
, and then, by induction hypothesis,
q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o}))
, hence
q=(o,(e_{o},q^{\prime}))\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(R\langle o,i,e_{o}\rangle)
; since
\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(q^{\prime})\notin\mathcal{P}_{R}^{\mathsf{f}}(o)
, we have
q\notin\mathsf{dom}(t_{\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e))})
and we obtain
q\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e))
;

–

or there exist
e_{o}\in e(o)
,
o^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o}))
and
q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(B_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})}(o^{\prime}))
such that
q=((o,(e_{o},o^{\prime})),q^{\prime})
and
\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(o^{\prime},q^{\prime})=p
, and then, by induction hypothesis,
(o^{\prime},q^{\prime})\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o}))
, hence
q=((o,(e_{o},o^{\prime})),q^{\prime})\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(R\langle o,i,e_{o}\rangle)
; since
\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(o^{\prime},q^{\prime})\notin\mathcal{P}_{R}^{\mathsf{f}}(o)
, we have
q\notin\mathsf{dom}(t_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)})
and we obtain
q\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e))
.

Conversely, let
q\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e))
. We distinguish between three cases:

•

q\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(R^{\leq i})
: we have
\kappa_{R}[i](e)(q)=q\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(R^{\leq i})\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e))
;

•

There exist
o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)
,
e_{o}\in e(o)
and
q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})))
such that
\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(q^{\prime})\notin\mathcal{P}_{R}^{\mathsf{f}}(o)
and
q=(o,(e_{o},q^{\prime}))
: by induction hypothesis, we have
\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(q^{\prime})\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(B_{R}(o))
, hence
\kappa_{R}[i](e)(q)=(o,\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(q^{\prime}))
with
\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(q^{\prime})\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(B_{R}(o))\setminus\mathcal{P}_{R}^{\mathsf{f}}(o)
; we thus have
\kappa_{R}[i](e)(q)\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(R)
.

•

There exist
o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)
,
e_{o}\in e(o)
,
o^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o}))
and
q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(B_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)}(o,(e_{o},o^{\prime})))\setminus\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)}^{\mathsf{f}}((o,(e_{o},o^{\prime})))
such that
\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(o^{\prime},q^{\prime})\notin\mathcal{P}_{R}^{\mathsf{f}}(o)
and
q=((o,(e_{o},o^{\prime})),q^{\prime})
: we have
q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(B_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})}(o^{\prime}))\setminus\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})}^{\mathsf{f}}(o^{\prime})
, hence
(o^{\prime},q^{\prime})\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o}))
; by induction hypothesis, we have
\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(o^{\prime},q^{\prime})\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(B_{R}(o))
; since
\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(o^{\prime},q^{\prime})\notin\mathcal{P}_{R}^{\mathsf{f}}(o)
, we have
\kappa_{R}[i](e)(q)=(o,\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(o^{\prime},q^{\prime}))\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(R)
.

{exa}

Generally, given a PS
R
and an integer
i
, the PS
\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)
does not depend only on
e^{\#}
(we can have
{e_{1}}^{\#}={e_{2}}^{\#}
and not
\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e_{1})\equiv\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e_{2})
), but with the PS
R
depicted in Figure
11
, it is not the case: with
this
PS
R
, we have
(\forall i\in\mathbb{N})(\forall e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathfrak{E}(R))({e_{1}}^{\#}={e_{2}}^{\#}\Rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e_{1})\equiv\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e_{2}))
. If
R
is this PS and
e
some pseudo-experiment such that
e^{\#}
is as described in Example
2
, then
\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e)=S0_{1}\oplus S0_{2}
, where
S0_{1}
and
S0_{2}
are the differential PS’s depicted in Figures
15
and
15
respectively, and
\mathcal{T}_{R}[1](e)=S1_{1}\oplus S1_{2}\oplus S1_{3}
, where
S1_{1}
,
S1_{2}
and
S1_{3}
are the differential PS’s depicted in Figures
18
,
18
and
18
respectively.
