Figure 18.
The differential PS
S1_{3}

The Taylor expansion we consider has no coefficients (i.e. has coefficients in the Boolean semiring
\mathbb{B}=\{0,1\}
, where
1+1=1
). In other words, we consider the support of Taylor expansion with coefficients:

{defi}

Let
R
be a PS. A
Taylor expansion of
R
is a set
\mathcal{T}_{R}[0]
of simple differential nets such that
\mathcal{T}_{R}[0]\equiv\{\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e);e\in\mathfrak{E}(R)\}
.

It is clear that two Taylor expansions of
R
are the same sets of simple differential nets up to the names of the ports that are not conclusions, that is why it makes sense to speak about “the” Taylor expansion of a PS.

An important case for our proof is the partial Taylor expansion of an in-PS of the form
\varphi\cdot_{o}R
for some in-PS
R
, some box
o
of
R
at depth
0
and some bijection
\varphi:\mathcal{P}_{R}^{\wn}(o)\simeq\mathcal{Q^{\prime}}
. Since we have
\mathfrak{E}(\varphi\cdot_{o}R)=\mathfrak{E}(R)
, we can compare
\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)
with
\mathcal{T}_{(\varphi\cdot_{o}R)}[i](e)
. From
B_{B_{(\varphi\cdot_{o}R)}(o)}=B_{B_{R}(o)}
, we already deduce
B_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{(\varphi\cdot_{o}R)}(o)}[i](e)}=B_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e)}
; more information is given by the following lemma:

Lemma 18
.

Let
R
be an in-PS. Let
o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(R)
. Let
e_{o}
be a pseudo-experiment on
B_{R}(o)
. Let
\varphi
be some bijection
\mathcal{P}_{R}^{\wn}(o)\simeq\mathcal{Q^{\prime}}
. Let
R_{o}
be an in-PS such that
R_{o}=\varphi\cdot_{o}R
. Let
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Then we have:

•

t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})}=\mathchoice{{t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})}\,\smash{\vrule height=4.92064pt,depth=8.3867pt}}_{\,\{p\in\mathsf{dom}(t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})});t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})}(p)\notin\mathcal{Q^{\prime}}\}}}{{t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})}\,\smash{\vrule height=4.92064pt,depth=8.3867pt}}_{\,\{p\in\mathsf{dom}(t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})});t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})}(p)\notin\mathcal{Q^{\prime}}\}}}{{t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.44446pt,depth=9.8506pt}}_{\,\{p\in\mathsf{dom}(t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})});t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})}(p)\notin\mathcal{Q^{\prime}}\}}}{{t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})}\,\smash{\vrule height=2.46031pt,depth=10.43616pt}}_{\,\{p\in\mathsf{dom}(t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})});t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})}(p)\notin\mathcal{Q^{\prime}}\}}}

•

and, for any
o_{1}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o}))
,
for any
p\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})}^{\mathsf{f}}(o_{1})
such that

t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})}(o_{1},p)\in\mathcal{Q^{\prime}},

we have

t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})}(o_{1},p)=\varphi(t_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)}((o,(e_{o},o_{1})),p))

Proof 2.2
.

We set

S={B_{R}(o)}^{\leq i}\oplus\bigoplus_{o^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}({B_{R}(o)})}\bigoplus_{e_{o^{\prime}}\in e_{o}(o^{\prime})}\langle o^{\prime},\langle e_{o^{\prime}},\mathcal{T}_{B_{B_{R}(o)}(o^{\prime})}[i](e_{o^{\prime}})\rangle\rangle

and

S_{o}={B_{R_{o}}(o)}^{\leq i}\oplus\bigoplus_{o^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}({B_{R_{o}}(o)})}\bigoplus_{e_{o^{\prime}}\in e_{o}(o^{\prime})}\langle o^{\prime},\langle e_{o^{\prime}},\mathcal{T}_{B_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}(o^{\prime})}[i](e_{o^{\prime}})\rangle\rangle

Notice that

S_{o}={B_{R_{o}}(o)}^{\leq i}\oplus\bigoplus_{o^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}({B_{R}(o)})}\bigoplus_{e_{o^{\prime}}\in e_{o}(o^{\prime})}\langle o^{\prime},\langle e_{o^{\prime}},\mathcal{T}_{B_{B_{R}(o)}(o^{\prime})}[i](e_{o^{\prime}})\rangle\rangle

and
\mathcal{B}_{0}(S_{o})=\mathcal{B}_{0}(S)
.
Let
p\in\mathsf{dom}(t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})})
and let us show that
p\in\mathsf{dom}(t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})})
. We distinguish between two cases:

•

p\in\mathsf{dom}(t_{{B_{R}(o)}^{\leq i}})
: Then
p\in\mathsf{dom}(t_{{B_{R_{o}}(o)}^{\leq i}})\subseteq\mathsf{dom}(t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})})
.

•

p\notin\mathsf{dom}(t_{{B_{R}(o)}^{\leq i}})
:
Then there exists
(o^{\prime},(e_{o^{\prime}},o^{\prime\prime}))\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(S)\setminus\mathcal{B}_{0}({B_{R}(o)}^{\leq i})=\mathcal{B}_{0}(S_{o})\setminus\mathcal{B}_{0}({B_{R_{o}}(o)}^{\leq i})
such that
p
is an element of the set

\displaystyle\left\{((o^{\prime},(e_{o^{\prime}},o^{\prime\prime})),q);\begin{array}[]{ll}((o^{\prime\prime},q)\in\mathcal{P}_{>0}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{B_{R}(o)}(o^{\prime})}[i](e_{o^{\prime}}))\\
\wedge\kappa_{B_{B_{R}(o)}(o^{\prime})}[i](e_{o^{\prime}})(o^{\prime\prime},q)\in\mathcal{P}_{B_{R}(o)}^{\mathsf{f}}(o^{\prime}))\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{((o^{\prime},(e_{o^{\prime}},o^{\prime\prime})),q);\begin{array}[]{ll}((o^{\prime\prime},q)\in\mathcal{P}_{>0}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}(o^{\prime})}[i](e_{o^{\prime}}))\\
\wedge\kappa_{B_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}(o^{\prime})}[i](e_{o^{\prime}})(o^{\prime\prime},q)\in\mathcal{P}_{B_{R}(o)}^{\mathsf{f}}(o^{\prime}))\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\subseteq

\displaystyle\left\{((o^{\prime},(e_{o^{\prime}},o^{\prime\prime})),q);\begin{array}[]{ll}((o^{\prime\prime},q)\in\mathcal{P}_{>0}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}(o^{\prime})}[i](e_{o^{\prime}}))\\
\wedge\kappa_{B_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}(o^{\prime})}[i](e_{o^{\prime}})(o^{\prime\prime},q)\in\mathcal{P}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}^{\mathsf{f}}(o^{\prime}))\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\subseteq

\displaystyle\mathsf{dom}(t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})})

Conversely, let
p\in\mathsf{dom}(t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})})
such that
t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})}(p)\notin\mathcal{Q^{\prime}}
; we will check that
p\in\mathsf{dom}(t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})})
and
t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})}(p)=t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})}(p)
. We distinguish between two cases:

•

p\in\mathsf{dom}(t_{{B_{R_{o}}(o)}^{\leq i}})
: Then
p\in\mathsf{dom}(t_{{({B_{R}(o)}\oplus\bigoplus_{q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q^{\prime}}}\wn_{q^{\prime}})}^{\leq i}})=\mathsf{dom}(t_{{{B_{R}(o)}}^{\leq i}})\subseteq\mathsf{dom}(t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})})
. In this case, we have
t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})}(p)=t_{B_{R}(o)}(p)=t_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}(p)=t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})}(p)
.

•

p\notin\mathsf{dom}(t_{{B_{R_{o}}(o)}^{\leq i}})
: Then there exists
(o^{\prime},(e_{o^{\prime}},o^{\prime\prime}))\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(S_{o})\setminus\mathcal{B}_{0}({B_{R_{o}}(o)}^{\leq i})=\mathcal{B}_{0}(S)\setminus\mathcal{B}_{0}({B_{R}(o)}^{\leq i})
such that
p
is an element of the set

\displaystyle\left\{((o^{\prime},(e_{o^{\prime}},o^{\prime\prime})),q);\begin{array}[]{ll}((o^{\prime\prime},q)\in\mathcal{P}_{>0}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}(o^{\prime})}[i](e_{o^{\prime}}))\\
\wedge\kappa_{B_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}(o^{\prime})}[i](e_{o^{\prime}})(o^{\prime\prime},q)\in\mathcal{P}_{{B_{R_{o}}(o)}}^{\mathsf{f}}(o^{\prime}))\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{((o^{\prime},(e_{o^{\prime}},o^{\prime\prime})),q);\begin{array}[]{ll}((o^{\prime\prime},q)\in\mathcal{P}_{>0}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{B_{R}(o)}(o^{\prime})}[i](e_{o^{\prime}}))\\
\wedge\kappa_{B_{B_{R}(o)}(o^{\prime})}[i](e_{o^{\prime}})(o^{\prime\prime},q)\in\mathcal{P}_{{B_{R_{o}}(o)}}^{\mathsf{f}}(o^{\prime}))\end{array}\right\}

(because
B_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}(o^{\prime})=B_{B_{R}(o)}(o^{\prime})
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{((o^{\prime},(e_{o^{\prime}},o^{\prime\prime})),q);\begin{array}[]{ll}((o^{\prime\prime},q)\in\mathcal{P}_{>0}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{B_{R}(o)}(o^{\prime})}[i](e_{o^{\prime}}))\\
\wedge\kappa_{B_{B_{R}(o)}(o^{\prime})}[i](e_{o^{\prime}})(o^{\prime\prime},q)\in\mathcal{P}_{{B_{R}(o)}}^{\mathsf{f}}(o^{\prime}))\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle\left\{((o^{\prime},(e_{o^{\prime}},o^{\prime\prime})),q);\begin{array}[]{ll}((o^{\prime\prime},q)\in\mathcal{P}_{>0}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{B_{R}(o)}(o^{\prime})}[i](e_{o^{\prime}}))\\
\wedge\kappa_{B_{B_{R}(o)}(o^{\prime})}[i](e_{o^{\prime}})(o^{\prime\prime},q)\in\mathcal{P}_{{B_{R_{o}}(o)}}^{\mathsf{f}}(o^{\prime})\\
\wedge t_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}(o^{\prime},\kappa_{B_{B_{R}(o)}(o^{\prime})}[i](e_{o^{\prime}})(o^{\prime\prime},q))\in\mathcal{Q^{\prime}})\end{array}\right\}

(by Remark
15
,
\mathcal{P}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}^{\mathsf{f}}(o^{\prime})=\mathcal{P}_{B_{R}(o)}^{\mathsf{f}}(o^{\prime})\cup\{q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}^{\mathsf{f}}(o^{\prime});t_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}(o^{\prime},q^{\prime})\in\mathcal{Q^{\prime}}\}
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{((o^{\prime},(e_{o^{\prime}},o^{\prime\prime})),q);\begin{array}[]{ll}((o^{\prime\prime},q)\in\mathcal{P}_{>0}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{B_{R}(o)}(o^{\prime})}[i](e_{o^{\prime}}))\\
\wedge\kappa_{B_{B_{R}(o)}(o^{\prime})}[i](e_{o^{\prime}})(o^{\prime\prime},q)\in\mathcal{P}_{{B_{R}(o)}}^{\mathsf{f}}(o^{\prime}))\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle\left\{((o^{\prime},(e_{o^{\prime}},o^{\prime\prime})),q);\begin{array}[]{ll}((o^{\prime\prime},q)\in\mathcal{P}_{>0}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{B_{R}(o)}(o^{\prime})}[i](e_{o^{\prime}}))\\
\wedge t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})}((o^{\prime},(e_{o^{\prime}},o^{\prime\prime})),q)\in\mathcal{Q^{\prime}})\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{((o^{\prime},(e_{o^{\prime}},o^{\prime\prime})),q);\begin{array}[]{ll}((o^{\prime\prime},q)\in\mathcal{P}_{>0}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{B_{R}(o)}(o^{\prime})}[i](e_{o^{\prime}}))\\
\wedge\kappa_{B_{B_{R}(o)}(o^{\prime})}[i](e_{o^{\prime}})(o^{\prime\prime},q)\in\mathcal{P}_{{B_{R}(o)}}^{\mathsf{f}}(o^{\prime}))\end{array}\right\}

(by assumption, we have
t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})}(p)\notin\mathcal{Q^{\prime}}
)

\displaystyle\subseteq

\displaystyle\mathsf{dom}(t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})})

In this case, if
q
is such that
p=((o^{\prime},(e_{o^{\prime}},o^{\prime\prime})),q)
, then we have

\displaystyle t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})}(p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t_{B_{R}(o)}(o^{\prime},\kappa_{B_{B_{R}(o)}(o^{\prime})}[i](e_{o^{\prime}})(o^{\prime\prime},q))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}(o^{\prime},\kappa_{B_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}(o^{\prime})}[i](e_{o^{\prime}})(o^{\prime\prime},q))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})}(p)

We thus have
t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})}=\mathchoice{{t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})}\,\smash{\vrule height=4.92064pt,depth=8.3867pt}}_{\,\{p\in\mathsf{dom}(t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})});t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})}(p)\notin\mathcal{Q^{\prime}}\}}}{{t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})}\,\smash{\vrule height=4.92064pt,depth=8.3867pt}}_{\,\{p\in\mathsf{dom}(t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})});t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})}(p)\notin\mathcal{Q^{\prime}}\}}}{{t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.44446pt,depth=9.8506pt}}_{\,\{p\in\mathsf{dom}(t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})});t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})}(p)\notin\mathcal{Q^{\prime}}\}}}{{t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})}\,\smash{\vrule height=2.46031pt,depth=10.43616pt}}_{\,\{p\in\mathsf{dom}(t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})});t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})}(p)\notin\mathcal{Q^{\prime}}\}}}
.

Now, let
o_{1}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o}))
and
p\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})}^{\mathsf{f}}(o_{1})
such that
t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})}(o_{1},p)\in\mathcal{Q^{\prime}}
.

•

If
o_{1}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{<i}(B_{R_{o}}(o))
, then
(o_{1},p)\in\mathcal{P}_{R}^{\mathsf{f}}(o)
, hence
t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})}(o_{1},p)=t_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}(o_{1},p)=\varphi(t_{R}(o,(o_{1},p)))=\varphi(t_{R}(o,\kappa_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})(o_{1},p)))
;

•

if
o_{1}=(o^{\prime},(e_{o^{\prime}},o^{\prime\prime}))
with
o^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(B_{R_{o}}(o))
, then
((o^{\prime},(e_{o^{\prime}},o^{\prime\prime})),p)\notin\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o}))
, hence, by Fact
17
,
(o^{\prime},\kappa_{B_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}(o^{\prime})}[i](e_{o^{\prime}})(o^{\prime\prime},p))=\kappa_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})((o^{\prime},(e_{o^{\prime}},o^{\prime\prime})),p)\notin\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(B_{R_{o}}(o))
. Moreover, since
t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})}(o_{1},p)\in\mathcal{Q^{\prime}}
, we have
(o^{\prime\prime},p)\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}(o^{\prime})}[i](e_{o^{\prime}}))
(otherwise,
t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}(o^{\prime})}[i](e_{o^{\prime}})}(o^{\prime\prime},p)\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}(o^{\prime})}[i](e_{o^{\prime}}))=\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{B_{R}(o)}(o^{\prime})}[i](e_{o^{\prime}}))
, which entails
t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})}(o_{1},p)\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{{B_{R}(o)}}[i](e_{o}))
, which contradicts
t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})}(o_{1},p)\in\mathcal{Q^{\prime}}
). By Fact
17
, we obtain
\kappa_{B_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}(o^{\prime})}[i](e_{o^{\prime}})(o^{\prime\prime},p)\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(B_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}(o^{\prime}))
. We showed
\kappa_{B_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}(o^{\prime})}[i](e_{o^{\prime}})(o^{\prime\prime},p)\in\mathcal{P}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}^{\mathsf{f}}(o^{\prime})
. We thus have

\displaystyle t_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})}(o_{1},p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}(o^{\prime},\kappa_{B_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}(o^{\prime})}[i](e_{o^{\prime}})(o^{\prime\prime},p))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi(t_{R}(o,(o^{\prime},\kappa_{B_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}(o^{\prime})}[i](e_{o^{\prime}})(o^{\prime\prime},p))))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi(t_{R}(o,\kappa_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})(o_{1},p)));

now, we have
t_{R}(o,\kappa_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})(o_{1},p))=t_{R}(o,\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(o_{1},p))=t_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)}((o,(e_{o},o_{1})),p)
.

The rest of this section is devoted to show Proposition
21
, which shows how one can compute, for any in-PS
R
, the arity in
\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)
of a port at depth
0
of
R
. For that purpose, we introduce the function
b_{S}^{\geq i}
that associates with every port
p
of
S
at depth greater than
i
the deepest box of depth at least
i
that contains
p
:

{defi}

For any differential in-PS
S
, for any
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we define, by induction on
\textit{depth}(S)
, the function
b_{S}^{\geq i}:\mathcal{P}_{>i}(S)\to\mathcal{B}^{\geq i}(S)
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{ccl}b_{S}^{\geq i}:\mathcal{P}_{>i}(S)&\to&\mathcal{B}^{\geq i}(S)\\
(o,p)&\mapsto&\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}o&\textit{if $o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(S)$ and $p\in\mathcal{P}_{\leq i}(B_{S}(o))$;}\\
(o,b_{B_{S}(o)}^{\geq i}(p))&\textit{if $o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(S)$ and $p\in\mathcal{P}_{>i}(B_{S}(o))$;}\end{array}\right.\end{array}

Fact 19
.

Let
R
be an in-PS. Let
e
be a pseudo-experiment on
R
. Let
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Then we have

(\forall p\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e))\setminus\mathcal{P}_{0}(R))(p\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e))\Leftrightarrow(b_{R}^{\geq 0}\circ\kappa_{R}[e](i))(p)\in\mathcal{B}^{\geq i}(R))

Proof 2.3
.

First notice that, for any differential in-PS
S
, we have:

(\forall p\in\mathcal{P}_{>0}(S))(b_{S}^{\geq 0}(p)\in\mathcal{B}^{\geq i}(S)\Rightarrow p\in\mathcal{P}_{>i}(S))\>\>\>(\ast)

Indeed: Let
p\in\mathcal{P}_{>0}(S)
such that
b_{S}^{\geq 0}(p)\in\mathcal{B}^{\geq i}(S)
and let
o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(S)
and
p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(B_{S}(o))
such that
p=(o,p^{\prime})
; we distinguish between two cases:

•

p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(B_{S}(o))
: We have
b_{S}^{\geq 0}(p)=o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(S)
;

•

p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{>0}(B_{S}(o))
: We have
b_{S}^{\geq 0}(p)=(o,b_{B_{S}(o)}^{\geq 0}(p^{\prime}))\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(S)
, hence
o\in\mathcal{B}^{\geq i}(S)
;

in both cases we have
o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(S)
, hence
p=(o,p^{\prime})\in\mathcal{P}_{>i}(S)
.

We prove now the fact by induction on
\textit{depth}(R)
. If
\textit{depth}(R)=0
, then
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e))\setminus\mathcal{P}_{0}(R)=\emptyset
. Otherwise, let
p\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e))\setminus\mathcal{P}_{0}(R)\not=\emptyset
:

•

If
p\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e))
, then there exist
o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)
,
e_{o}\in e(o)
and
p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o}))
such that
p=(o,(e_{o},p^{\prime}))
; we distinguish between two cases:

–

p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(B_{R}(o))
: We have
b_{R}^{\geq 0}(\kappa_{R}[i](e)(p))=b_{R}^{\geq 0}(o,p^{\prime})=o\in\mathcal{B}^{\geq i}(R)
.

–

p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{>0}(B_{R}(o))
: By induction hypothesis, we have
b_{B_{R}(o)}^{\geq 0}(\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(p^{\prime}))\in\mathcal{B}^{\geq i}(B_{R}(o))
, hence, by
(\ast)
,
\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(p^{\prime})\in\mathcal{P}_{>i}(B_{R}(o))
; we thus have

b_{R}^{\geq 0}(\kappa_{R}[i](e)(p))=b_{R}^{\geq 0}(o,\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(p^{\prime}))=(o,b_{B_{R}(o)}^{\geq 0}(\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(p^{\prime})))\in\mathcal{B}^{\geq i}(R).

•

If
p\in\mathcal{P}_{>0}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e))
, then there exist
o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e))
and
p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(B_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)}(o))
such that
p=(o,p^{\prime})
; we distinguish between two cases:

–

o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{<i}(R)
: Then
p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{\leq i}(B_{R}(o))
, hence
b_{R}^{\geq 0}(\kappa_{R}[i](e)(p))=b_{R}^{\geq 0}(o,p^{\prime})=o\in\mathcal{B}^{<i}(R)
;

–

o=(o_{1},(e_{1},o^{\prime}))
with
o_{1}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)
,
e_{1}\in e(o_{1})
and
o^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o_{1})}[i](e_{1}))
: By induction hypothesis, we have
b_{B_{R}(o_{1})}^{\geq 0}(\kappa_{B_{R}(o_{1})}[i](e_{1})(o^{\prime},p^{\prime}))\in\mathcal{B}^{<i}(B_{R}(o_{1}))
, hence

b_{R}^{\geq 0}(\kappa_{R}[i](e)(p))=b_{R}^{\geq 0}(o_{1},\kappa_{B_{R}(o_{1})}[i](e_{1})(o^{\prime},p^{\prime}))=(o_{1},b_{B_{R}(o_{1})}^{\geq 0}(\kappa_{B_{R}(o_{1})}[i](e_{1})(o^{\prime},p^{\prime})))\in\mathcal{B}^{<i}(R).

If a port
q
of a PS
R
is deep enough, then it is duplicated
\sum e^{\#}(b_{R}^{\geq i}(q))
times in
\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)
:

Lemma 20
.

Let
R
be an in-PS. Let
e
be a pseudo-experiment on
R
. Let
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
q\in\mathcal{P}_{>i}(R)
. Then we have

\mathsf{Card}\left(\{p\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e));\kappa_{R}[i](e)(p)=q\}\right)=\sum e^{\#}(b_{R}^{\geq i}(q))

Proof 2.4
.

By induction on
\textit{depth}(R)
. If
\textit{depth}(R)=0
, then there is no such
q
. Otherwise: Let
o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)
and
q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(B_{R}(o))
such that
q=(o,q^{\prime})
. We distinguish between three cases:

•

q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{\leq i}(B_{R}(o))
: we have
b_{R}^{\geq 0}(q)=b_{R}^{\geq 0}(o,q^{\prime})=o\in\mathcal{B}^{\geq i}(R)
, hence, by Fact
19
, we have
\{p\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e));\kappa_{R}[i](e)(p)=q\}=\{p\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e))\setminus\mathcal{P}_{0}(R);\kappa_{R}[i](e)(p)=(o,q^{\prime})\}=\{(o,(e_{o},q^{\prime}));e_{o}\in e(o)\}
, hence

\displaystyle\mathsf{Card}\left(\{p\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e));\kappa_{R}[i](e)(p)=q\}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsf{Card}\left(e(o)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum e^{\#}(o)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum e^{\#}(b_{R}^{\geq i}(q))

•

q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{>i}(B_{R}(o))
and
b_{B_{R}(o)}^{\geq 0}(q^{\prime})\in\mathcal{B}^{\geq i}(B_{R}(o))
: we have
b_{R}^{\geq 0}(q)=b_{R}^{\geq 0}(o,q^{\prime})=(o,b_{B_{R}(o)}^{\geq 0}(q^{\prime}))\in\mathcal{B}^{\geq i}(R)
, hence, by Fact
19
,
\{p\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e));\kappa_{R}[i](e)(p)=q\}=\{p\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e))\setminus\mathcal{P}_{0}(R);\kappa_{R}[i](e)(p)=(o,q^{\prime})\}=\bigcup_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\{(o,(e_{o},p^{\prime}));(p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o}))\wedge\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(p^{\prime})=q^{\prime})\}
; for any
e_{o}\in e(o)
, by Fact
19
again, we have

\{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o}));\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(p^{\prime})=q^{\prime}\}=\{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o}));\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(p^{\prime})=q^{\prime}\}

and, by induction hypothesis, we have

\mathsf{Card}\left(\{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o}));\kappa_{e_{o},i}(p^{\prime})=q^{\prime}\}\right)=\sum{e_{o}}^{\#}(b_{B_{R}(o)}^{\geq i}(q^{\prime}))

We thus obtain

\displaystyle\mathsf{Card}\left(\{p\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e));\kappa_{R}[i](e)(p)=q\}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsf{Card}\left(\bigcup_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\{(o,(e_{o},p^{\prime}));(p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o}))\wedge\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(p^{\prime})=q^{\prime})\}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\mathsf{Card}\left(\{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o}));\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(p^{\prime})=q^{\prime}\}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\sum{e_{o}}^{\#}(b_{B_{R}(o)}^{\geq i}(q^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum e^{\#}(o,b_{B_{R}(o)}^{\geq i}(q^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum e^{\#}(b_{R}^{\geq i}(q))

•

q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{>i}(B_{R}(o))
and
b_{B_{R}(o)}^{\geq 0}(q^{\prime})\in\mathcal{B}^{<i}(B_{R}(o))
: we have
b_{R}^{\geq 0}(q)=b_{R}^{\geq 0}(o,q^{\prime})=(o,b_{B_{R}(o)}^{\geq 0}(q^{\prime}))\in\mathcal{B}^{<i}(R)
, hence, by Fact
19
,

\{p\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e));\kappa_{R}[i](e)(p)=q\}=\{p\in\mathcal{P}_{>0}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e));\kappa_{R}[i](e)(p)=(o,q^{\prime})\};

for any
e_{o}\in e(o)
, by Fact
19
again, we have
\{p\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o}));\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(p)=q^{\prime}\}=\{p\in\mathcal{P}_{>0}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o}));\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(p)=q^{\prime}\}
and, by induction hypothesis, we have

\mathsf{Card}\left(\{p\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o}));\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(p)=q^{\prime}\}\right)=\sum{e_{o}}^{\#}(b_{B_{R}(o)}^{\geq i}(q^{\prime}))

hence

\displaystyle\mathsf{Card}\left(\{p\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e));\kappa_{R}[i](e)(p)=q\}\right)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\mathsf{Card}\left(\bigcup_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\{((o,(e_{o},o^{\prime})),p^{\prime});((o^{\prime},p^{\prime})\in\mathcal{P}_{>0}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o}))\wedge\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(o^{\prime},p^{\prime})=q^{\prime})\}\right)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\mathsf{Card}\left(\{p\in\mathcal{P}_{>0}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o}));\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(p)=q^{\prime}\}\right)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\mathsf{Card}\left(\{p\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o}));\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(p)=q^{\prime}\}\right)

(because
(\forall e_{o}\in e(o))(\forall p\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})))\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(p)\notin\mathcal{P}_{>i}(B_{R}(o))
)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\sum{e_{o}}^{\#}(b_{B_{R}(o)}^{\geq i}(q^{\prime}))

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum e^{\#}(o,b_{B_{R}(o)}^{\geq i}(q^{\prime}))

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum e^{\#}(b_{R}^{\geq i}(q))

We finally obtain Proposition
21
:

Proposition 21
.

Let
R
be an in-PS. Let
e
be a pseudo-experiment on
R
. Let
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
p\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(R)
. Then we have

{\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)}(p)={\textit{a}}_{R^{\leq i}}(p)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{>i}(R)\\
t_{R}(p^{\prime})=p\end{subarray}}\sum e^{\#}(b_{R}^{\geq i}(p^{\prime}))

Proof 2.5
.

We have

\displaystyle\sum_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)}\sum_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\mathsf{Card}\left(\left\{q\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})));\begin{array}[]{l}(\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(q)\in\mathcal{P}_{R}^{\mathsf{f}}(o)\\
\wedge t_{R}(o,\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(q))=p)\end{array}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)}\sum_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{R}^{\mathsf{f}}(o)\\
t_{R}(o,p^{\prime})=p\end{subarray}}\mathsf{Card}\left(\left\{q\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})));\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(q)=p^{\prime}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)}\sum_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{R}^{\mathsf{f}}(o)\\
t_{R}(o,p^{\prime})=p\end{subarray}}\mathsf{Card}\left(\left\{q\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o}));\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(q)=p^{\prime}\right\}\right)

(by Fact
17
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)}\sum_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{R}^{\mathsf{f}}(o)\\
t_{R}(o,p^{\prime})=p\end{subarray}}\mathsf{Card}\left(\left\{q\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o}));\kappa_{R}[i](e)(o,(e_{o},q))=(o,p^{\prime})\right\}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{R}^{\mathsf{f}}(o)\\
t_{R}(o,p^{\prime})=p\end{subarray}}\sum_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\mathsf{Card}\left(\left\{q\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o}));\kappa_{R}[i](e)(o,(e_{o},q))=(o,p^{\prime})\right\}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{>i}(R)\\
t_{R}(p^{\prime})=p\end{subarray}}\mathsf{Card}\left(\{q\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e));\kappa_{R}[i](e)(q)=p^{\prime}\}\right)

and, for any
o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)
, for any
e_{o}\in e(o)
, we set

\displaystyle d_{o}(e_{o})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{o^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o}))}\mathsf{Card}\left(\left\{(o^{\prime},q)\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o}));\begin{array}[]{l}(\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(o^{\prime},q)\in\mathcal{P}_{R}^{\mathsf{f}}(o)\\
\wedge t_{R}(o,\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(o^{\prime},q))=p)\end{array}\right\}\right)

we have

\displaystyle d_{o}(e_{o})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{o^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o}))}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{R}^{\mathsf{f}}(o)\\
t_{R}(o,q^{\prime})=p\end{subarray}}\mathsf{Card}\left(\left\{(o^{\prime},q)\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o}));\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(o^{\prime},q)=q^{\prime}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{o^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o}))}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{R}^{\mathsf{f}}(o)\\
t_{R}(o,q^{\prime})=p\end{subarray}}\mathsf{Card}\left(\left\{(o^{\prime},q)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o}));\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(o^{\prime},q)=q^{\prime}\right\}\right)

(by Fact
17
)

hence

\displaystyle\sum_{e_{o}\in e(o)}d_{o}(e_{o})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{R}^{\mathsf{f}}(o)\\
t_{R}(o,q^{\prime})=p\end{subarray}}\sum_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\sum_{o^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o}))}\mathsf{Card}\left(\left\{(o^{\prime},q)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o}));\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(o^{\prime},q)=q^{\prime}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{R}^{\mathsf{f}}(o)\\
t_{R}(o,q^{\prime})=p\end{subarray}}\sum_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\sum_{o^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o}))}\mathsf{Card}\left(\left\{(o^{\prime},q)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o}));\kappa_{R}[i](e)((o,(e_{o},o^{\prime})),q)=(o,q^{\prime})\right\}\right)

and

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)\end{subarray}}\sum_{e_{o}\in e(o)}d_{o}(e_{o})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{>i}(R)\\
t_{R}(p^{\prime})=p\end{subarray}}\mathsf{Card}\left(\{q\in\mathcal{P}_{>0}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e));\kappa_{R}[i](e)(q)=p^{\prime}\}\right)

We thus have

\displaystyle{\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)}(p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\textit{a}}_{R^{\leq i}}(p)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)\end{subarray}}\sum_{e_{o}\in e(o)}d_{o}(e_{o})

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)}\sum_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\mathsf{Card}\left(\left\{q\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})));\begin{array}[]{l}(\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(q)\in\mathcal{P}_{R}^{\mathsf{f}}(o)\\
\wedge t_{R}(o,\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})(q))=p)\end{array}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\textit{a}}_{R^{\leq i}}(p)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{>i}(R)\\
t_{R}(p^{\prime})=p\end{subarray}}\mathsf{Card}\left(\{q\in\mathcal{P}_{>0}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e));\kappa_{R}[i](e)(q)=p^{\prime}\}\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{>i}(R)\\
t_{R}(p^{\prime})=p\end{subarray}}\mathsf{Card}\left(\{q\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e));\kappa_{R}[i](e)(q)=p^{\prime}\}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\textit{a}}_{R^{\leq i}}(p)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{>i}(R)\\
t_{R}(p^{\prime})=p\end{subarray}}\mathsf{Card}\left(\{q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e));\kappa_{R}[i](e)(q)=p^{\prime}\}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\textit{a}}_{R^{\leq i}}(p)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{>i}(R)\\
t_{R}(p^{\prime})=p\end{subarray}}\sum e^{\#}(b_{R}^{\geq i}(p^{\prime}))

(by Lemma
20
)

{exa}

Consider the port
p_{1}
of
R
at depth
0
(see Figure
11
): We have
\{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{>1}(R);t_{R}(p^{\prime})=p_{1}\}=\{(o_{2},q^{\prime})\}
,
b_{R}^{\geq 1}((o_{2},q^{\prime}))=o_{2}
and
{\textit{a}}_{R^{\leq 1}}(p_{1})=1
(see Figure

, p.
35
). Now, if
e
is a pseudo-experiment as in Example
2
, then
e^{\#}(o_{2})=\{10\}
, hence
{\textit{a}}_{R^{\leq 1}}(p_{1})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{>1}(R)\\
t_{R}(p^{\prime})=p_{1}\end{subarray}}\sum e^{\#}(b_{R}^{\geq 1}(p^{\prime}))=1+\sum\{10\}=11
. And, indeed, we have
{\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[1](e)}(p_{1})=11
(see Figure
18
).

Corollary 22
.

Let
R
be an in-PS. Let
o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(R)
. Let
\varphi
be some bijection
\mathcal{P}_{R}^{\wn}(o)\simeq\mathcal{Q^{\prime}}
and let
R_{o}
be an in-PS such that
R_{o}=\varphi\cdot_{o}R
. Let
e_{o}\in e(o)
. Let
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, for any
p\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o}))
, we have
{\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})}(p)={\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})}(p)
.

Proof 2.6
.

Let
p\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o}))
. If
p\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(B_{R}(o))
, then, by Proposition
21
, we have

\displaystyle{\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})}(p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\textit{a}}_{{B_{R}(o)}^{\leq i}}(p)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{>i}(B_{R}(o))\\
t_{B_{R}(o)}(p^{\prime})=p\end{subarray}}\sum{e_{o}}^{\#}(b_{B_{R}(o)}^{\geq i}(p^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\textit{a}}_{{B_{R_{o}}(o)}^{\leq i}}(p)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{>i}(B_{R_{o}}(o))\\
t_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}(p^{\prime})=p\end{subarray}}\sum{e_{o}}^{\#}(b_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}^{\geq i}(p^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})}(p)

If
p\notin\mathcal{P}_{0}(B_{R}(o))
, then there exist
o_{1}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(B_{R}(o))
,
e_{1}\in e_{o}(o_{1})
and
p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{B_{R}(o)}(o_{1})}[i](e_{1}))
such that
p=(o_{1},(e_{1},p^{\prime}))
; moreover,

\displaystyle{\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})}(p)={}

\displaystyle{\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{B_{R}(o)}(o_{1})}[i](e_{1})}(p^{\prime})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle{\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}(o_{1})}[i](e_{1})}(p^{\prime})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle{\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R_{o}}(o)}[i](e_{o})}(p)

3.
Rebuilding the proof-structure

The Taylor expansion of a PS is an infinite set of simple differential nets (for PS’s of depth
>0
). It was not known whether from this infinite set is was possible to rebuild the PS; indeed,
a priori
, two different PS’s could have the same Taylor expansion. We will not only show that it is possible to rebuild any PS
R
from its Taylor expansion
\mathcal{T}_{R}[0]
, we will also show something much stronger: We are already able to rebuild the PS with only one well-chosen simple differential net that appears in the Taylor expansion, chosen according to a specific information given by a second simple differential net of the Taylor expansion.

The algorithm leading from the simple differential net
\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e)
for some well-chosen pseudo-experiment
e
on
R
to the entire rebuilding of
R
is done in several steps: In the intermediate steps, we obtain a partial rebuilding where some boxes have been recovered but not all of them; a convenient way to represent this information is to use differential PS’s, which lie between the purely linear differential proof-nets and the non-linear proof-nets.

The rebuilding of the PS
R
is done in
d
steps, where
d
is the depth of
R
. We first rebuild the occurrences of the boxes of depth
0
(the deepest ones) and next we rebuild the occurrences of the boxes of depth
1
and so on… This can be formalized using simple differential nets (possibly with boxes) as follows: starting from
\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e)
, the first step of the algorithm builds
\mathcal{T}_{R}[1](e)
, the second step builds
\mathcal{T}_{R}[2](e)
from
\mathcal{T}_{R}[1](e)
, and so on… until
\mathcal{T}_{R}[\textit{depth}(R)](e)=R
. We thus reduced the problem of rebuilding the PS to the problem of rebuilding
\mathcal{T}_{R}[i+1](e)
from
\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)
for some well-chosen pseudo-experiment
e
.

We can hardly obtain much more than the following property for a non-well-chosen pseudo-experiment:

Lemma 23
.

Let
R
be an in-PS. Let
e
be a pseudo-experiment on
R
. Let
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Then we have:

•

{\mathcal{T}_{R}[i+1](e)}^{\leq i}\sqsubseteq_{\emptyset}{\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)}

•

(\forall o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{<i}({\mathcal{T}_{R}[i+1](e)}))\mathcal{P}_{{\mathcal{T}_{R}[i+1](e)}}^{\mathsf{f}}(o)=\mathcal{P}_{{\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)}}^{\mathsf{f}}(o)
;

•

and
\mathchoice{{\kappa_{R}[i+1](e)\,\smash{\vrule height=6.00002pt,depth=3.02223pt}}_{\,\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i+1](e))}}{{\kappa_{R}[i+1](e)\,\smash{\vrule height=6.00002pt,depth=3.02223pt}}_{\,\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i+1](e))}}{{\kappa_{R}[i+1](e)\,\smash{\vrule height=4.20001pt,depth=2.57834pt}}_{\,\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i+1](e))}}{{\kappa_{R}[i+1](e)\,\smash{\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.1639pt}}_{\,\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i+1](e))}}=\mathchoice{{\kappa_{R}[i](e)\,\smash{\vrule height=6.00002pt,depth=3.02223pt}}_{\,\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i+1](e))}}{{\kappa_{R}[i](e)\,\smash{\vrule height=6.00002pt,depth=3.02223pt}}_{\,\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i+1](e))}}{{\kappa_{R}[i](e)\,\smash{\vrule height=4.20001pt,depth=2.57834pt}}_{\,\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i+1](e))}}{{\kappa_{R}[i](e)\,\smash{\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.1639pt}}_{\,\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i+1](e))}}
.

Proof 3.1
.

By induction on
\textit{depth}(R)
, noticing, by applying Remark
13
, Remark
12
and Remark
4
, that we have
{\mathcal{T}_{R}[i+1](e)}^{\leq i}=S@t
with

S=R^{\leq i}\oplus\bigoplus_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i+1}(R)}\bigoplus_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\langle o,\langle e_{o},\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i+1](e_{o})^{\leq i}\rangle\rangle

and
t
is the function
\mathcal{W}_{0}\cup\mathcal{W}_{>0}\to\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\textit{e}}(R)
, where

•

\mathcal{W}_{0}=\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i+1}(R)}\bigcup_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\{(o,(e_{o},q));(q\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i+1](e_{o})))\wedge\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i+1](e_{o})(q)\in\mathcal{P}_{R}^{\mathsf{f}}(o))\}

•

\mathcal{W}_{>0}=\bigcup_{(o,(e_{o},o^{\prime}))\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{<i}(S)\setminus\mathcal{B}_{0}(R^{\leq i+1})}\left\{((o,(e_{o},o^{\prime})),q);\begin{array}[]{ll}((o^{\prime},q)\in\mathcal{P}_{>0}^{\mathsf{f}}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i+1](e_{o}))\\
\wedge\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i+1](e_{o})(o^{\prime},q)\in\mathcal{P}_{R}^{\mathsf{f}}(o))\end{array}\right\}

•

and
t(p)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}t_{R}(o,\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i+1](e_{o})(q))&\textit{if $p=(o,(e_{o},q))\in\mathcal{W}_{0}$;}\\
t_{R}(o,\kappa_{B_{R}(o)}[i+1](e_{o})(o^{\prime},q))&\textit{if $p=((o,(e_{o},o^{\prime})),q)\in\mathcal{W}_{>0}$.}\end{array}\right.

We thus have to consider some special experiments. As a first requirement, the pseudo-experiments we will consider are
exhaustive
:

{defi}

A pseudo-experiment
e
of an in-PS
R
is said to be
exhaustive
if, for any
o\in\mathcal{B}(R)
, we have
0\notin e^{\#}(o)
.

But this requirement is not strong enough. More specific pseudo-experiments have to be considered.

•

In
[
35
]
, it was shown that given the result of an
injective
k
-obsessional experiment
(
k
big enough) of a cut-free proof-net in the fragment
A::=X|\wn A\invamp A|A\invamp\wn A|A\otimes A|!A
, one can rebuild the entire experiment and, so, the entire proof-net. We recall that the LPS of a cut-free proof-net forgets the outline of the boxes but keeps the trace of the auxiliary doors (see Figure
22
, p.
22
for an example). There, “injective” means that the experiment labels two different axioms with different atoms and “obsessional” means that different copies of the same axiom are labeled by the same atom. Obsessionality entails that the names of the atoms play some role and thus we cannot reduce such experiments to some pseudo-experiments.

•

In
[
13
]
, it was shown that, for any two cut-free MELL proof-nets
R
and
R^{\prime}
, we have
LPS(R)=LPS(R^{\prime})
if, and only if, for
k
big enough
25
25

25

Interestingly,
[
20
]
, following the approach of
[
13
]
, showed that, if these two proof-nets are assumed to be (recursively) connected, then we can take
k=2
.
, there exist an
injective
k
-experiment
on
R
and an
injective
k
-experiment
on
R^{\prime}
having the same result; as an immediate corollary we obtained the injectivity of the set of (recursively) connected proof-nets. There, “injective” means that not only the experiment labels two different axioms with different atoms, but it labels also different copies of the same axiom by different atoms. Given some proof-net
R
, there is exactly one injective
k
-experiment on
R
up to the names of the atoms. Injectivity allows to reduce such experiments to some pseudo-experiments: it makes sense to define
k
-pseudo-experiments
and we could show in the same way that, for any two cut-free proof-nets
R
and
R^{\prime}
, the two following statements are equivalent:

–

we have
LPS(R)=LPS(R^{\prime})
;

–

for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, for any
k
-pseudo-experiment
e
on
R
, for any
k
-pseudo-experiment
e^{\prime}
on
R^{\prime}
, we have
\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e)=\mathcal{T}_{R^{\prime}}[0](e^{\prime})
, where a
k
-pseudo-experiment
is a pseudo-experiment such that, for any
o\in\mathcal{B}(R)
, we have
e^{\#}(o)=\{k\}
.

Now, there are many different cut-free PS’s with the same LPS (see Figures
22
,
26
,
26
,
26
and
26
, p.
22
for an example).

In
[
13
]
, the interest for
injective
experiments came from the remark that the result of an (atomic)
injective
experiment on a
cut-free
proof-net can be easily identified with a simple differential net of its Taylor expansion in a sum of simple differential nets
[
17
]
(it is essentially the content of our Lemma
61
). Thus any proof using injective experiments can be straightforwardly expressed in terms of simple differential nets and conversely. Since this identification is trivial, besides the idea of considering injective experiments instead of obsessional experiments, the use of the terminology of differential nets does not bring any new insight
26
26

26

For proof-nets with cuts, the situation is completely different: the great novelty of differential nets is that differential nets have a cut-elimination; the simple differential nets appearing in the Taylor expansion of a proof-net with cuts have cuts, while the semantics does not see these cuts. But the proofs of the injectivity only consider cut-free proof-nets.
, it just superficially changes the presentation.That is why we decided in
[
13
]
to avoid introducing explicitly differential nets. In
[
10
]
, we made the opposite choice for the following reason: The simple differential net representing the result and the proof-net are both instances of the more general notion of “simple differential nets possibly with boxes”, which are used to represent the partial information obtained during the algorithm execution. Moreover, this identification allows to see the injectivity of the relational semantics as a particular case of the invertibility of Taylor expansion.

In the present paper, we introduce the notion of
k
-heterogeneous
(pseudo-)
experiment
(
k
-heterogeneous pseudo-experiments on
cut-free
PS’s are an abstraction of injective
k
-heterogeneous experiments, where
injective
has the same meaning as in
[
13
]
) and the simple differential net we will consider to rebuild entirely the PS is the simple differential net obtained by expanding the boxes according to any
k
-heterogeneous pseudo-experiment
(for
k
big enough): We show that, for any cut-free PS
R
, given the result
\alpha
of a
k
-heterogeneous experiment
on
R
for
k
big enough, if
\alpha\in\llbracket R^{\prime}\rrbracket
, where
R^{\prime}
is any cut-free PS, then
R^{\prime}
is the same PS as
R
. The constraints on
k
are given by the result of a
1
-experiment, so we show that two (well-chosen) points are enough to determine a PS. The expression “
k
-heterogeneous” means that, for any two different occurrences of boxes, the experiment never takes the same number of copies: it takes
k^{j_{1}}
copies and
k^{j_{2}}
copies with
j_{1}\not=j_{2}
(
a contrario
, in
[
35
]
and
[
13
]
, the experiments always take the same number of copies).

As shown by the proof-net

: The experiment

and

.

Actually, more generally, we show that, for
any
PS
R
, given the simple differential net
\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e)
that belongs to the support of the Taylor expansion and that has been obtained by expanding the boxes according to any (atomic) injective
k
-heterogeneous pseudo-experiment
e
on

for
k
big enough, if
\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e)
belongs to the support of the Taylor expansion of any PS
R^{\prime}
, then
R^{\prime}
is the same PS as

. Notice that in presence of cuts, the
k
-heterogeneous pseudo-experiment we consider is not necessarily induced (see Definition
4.1
) by an experiment.
27
27

27

In the case there is no such experiment, the simple differential net
\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e)
reduces to
0
.

{defi}

Let
k>0
. A pseudo-experiment
e
on an in-PS
R
is said to be
k
-heterogeneous
if

•

for any
o\in\mathcal{B}(R)
, for any
m\in e^{\#}(o)
, there exists
j>0
such that
m=k^{j}
;

•

for any
o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(R)
, for any
o^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}(B_{R}(o))
, we have
(\forall e_{1},e_{2}\in e(o))

({e_{1}}^{\#}(o^{\prime})\cap{e_{2}}^{\#}(o^{\prime})\not=\emptyset\Rightarrow e_{1}=e_{2})
;

•

and, for any
o_{1},o_{2}\in\mathcal{B}(R)
, we have
({e}^{\#}(o_{1})\cap{e}^{\#}(o_{2})\not=\emptyset\Rightarrow o_{1}=o_{2})
.

{exa}

The pseudo-experiment
e
of Example
2
is a
10
-heterogeneous pseudo-experiment.

k
-heterogeneous experiments are characterized by (the arities of the co-contractions of) their corresponding terms of the Taylor expansion:

Lemma 24
.

For any in-PS
R
, we have
\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}(R)}e^{\#}(o)={\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e)}[\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\oc}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e))]
.

Proof 3.2
.

By Proposition
21
, we have
\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(R)}e^{\#}(o)={\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e)}[\mathcal{B}_{0}(R)]
, hence

\displaystyle\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}(R)}e^{\#}(o)={}

\displaystyle\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(R)}e^{\#}(o)\cup\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(R)}\bigcup_{o^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}(B_{R}(o))}e^{\#}(o,o^{\prime})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle{\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e)}[\mathcal{B}_{0}(R)]\cup\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(R)}\bigcup_{o^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}(B_{R}(o))}\bigcup_{e_{o}\in e(o)}{e_{o}}^{\#}(o^{\prime})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle{\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e)}[\mathcal{B}_{0}(R)]\cup\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(R)}\bigcup_{e_{o}\in e(o)}{\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[0](e_{o})}[\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\oc}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[0](e_{o}))]

(by induction hypothesis)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle{\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e)}[\mathcal{B}_{0}(R)]\cup\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(R)}\bigcup_{e_{o}\in e(o)}{\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e)}[\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\oc}(R\langle o,0,e_{o}\rangle)]

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle{\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e)}[\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\oc}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e))]

Corollary 25
.

Let
R
be an in-PS. Let
e
be a pseudo-experiment on
R
. Let
k>1
. Then
e
is a
k
-heterogeneous pseudo-experiment on
R
if, and only if, the two following properties hold together:

(1)

{\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e)}[\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\oc}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e))]\subseteq\{k^{j};j>0\}

(2)

(\forall p_{1},p_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\oc}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e)))({\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e)}(p_{1})={\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e)}(p_{2})\Rightarrow p_{1}=p_{2})

Proof 3.3
.

For any pseudo-experiment
e
on
R
, applying Lemma
24
, we obtain:

•

For any
k>1
, one has
(\forall o\in\mathcal{B}(R))(\forall m\in e^{\#}(o))(\exists j>0)m=k^{j}
if, and only if, one has
{\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e)}[\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\oc}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e))]\subseteq\{k^{j};j>0\}
.

•

One has
(\forall o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(R))(\forall o^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}(B_{R}(o)))(\forall e_{1},e_{2}\in e(o))({e_{1}}^{\#}(o^{\prime})\cap{e_{2}}^{\#}(o^{\prime})\not=\emptyset\Rightarrow e_{1}=e_{2})
if, and only if, one has
(\forall o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(R))(\forall e_{1},e_{2}\in e(o))

({\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e)}[\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\oc}(R\langle o,0,e_{1}\rangle)]\cap{\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e)}[\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\oc}(R\langle o,0,e_{2}\rangle)]\not=\emptyset\Rightarrow e_{1}=e_{2})
.

•

One has
(\forall o_{1},o_{2}\in\mathcal{B}(R))({e}^{\#}(o_{1})\cap{e}^{\#}(o_{2})\not=\emptyset\Rightarrow o_{1}=o_{2})
if, and only if, one has
(\forall p_{1},p_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\oc}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e)))({\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e)}(p_{1})={\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e)}(p_{2})\Rightarrow\kappa_{R}[0](e)(p_{1})=\kappa_{R}[0](e)(p_{2}))
.

We prove, by induction on
\textit{depth}(R)
, that, for any
k>1
, the two following properties together:

•

(\forall o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(R))(\forall e_{1},e_{2}\in e(o))

({\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e)}[\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\oc}(R\langle o,0,e_{1}\rangle)]\cap{\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e)}[\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\oc}(R\langle o,0,e_{2}\rangle)]\not=\emptyset\Rightarrow e_{1}=e_{2})

•

and
(\forall p_{1},p_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\oc}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e)))({\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e)}(p_{1})={\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e)}(p_{2})\Rightarrow\kappa_{R}[0](e)(p_{1})=\kappa_{R}[0](e)(p_{2}))

imply
(\forall p_{1},p_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\oc}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e)))({\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e)}(p_{1})={\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e)}(p_{2})\Rightarrow p_{1}=p_{2})
; the converse is trivial.

We will rebuild
\mathcal{T}_{R}[i+1](e)
from
\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)
for any
k
-heterogeneous pseudo-experiment
e
on
R
(with
k\geq\beta(R)
, where
\beta(R)
is an integer
28
28

28

The integer
\beta(R)
is defined in Definition
3.2
.
provided by any
1
-pseudo-experiment on
R
). For this purpose we will introduce our notion of
critical component
(elements of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)}^{k}(\mathcal{K}_{k,j}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)))
with
j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}(e)
)
29
29

29

The set
\mathcal{N}_{i}(e)
is a set of integers that will be defined in Definition
3.1
, the set
\mathcal{K}_{k,j}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e))
is a set of exponential ports of
\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)
at depth
0
that will be defined in Definition
3.1.2
and the sets
\mathcal{S}_{S}^{k}(\mathcal{Q})
of

T
of

that are connected
via
other ports than
\mathcal{Q}
, whose conclusions belong to
\mathcal{Q}
and with
\textit{co-size}(T)<k
will be defined in Definition
3.2
.
, which are special connected components of
\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)
,
connected
in a weak sense, and we will consider equivalence classes of critical components of
\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)
for the relation
\equiv
that forgets names of ports apart from those of shallow conclusions.
30
30

30

This relation has been defined in Definition
1.2
.
We can summarize the three main ideas of our proof as follows:

(1)

The simple differential net that corresponds to a
k
-heterogeneous pseudo-experiments is informative enough to rebuild the entire PS if
k
is big enough.

(2)

We introduce the notion of
partial
Taylor expansion
\mathcal{T}_{R}[i]
of a PS
R
(with
i\in\mathbb{N}
) and reduce the problem of the rebuilding of a PS
R
to the problem of the rebuilding of the simple differential net
possibly with boxes

\mathcal{T}_{R}[i+1](e)
that corresponds to a
k
-heterogeneous pseudo-experiment
e
in the partial Taylor expansion
\mathcal{T}_{R}[i]
from the simple differential net
possibly with boxes

\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)
that corresponds to the same
k
-heterogeneous pseudo-experiment
e
in the partial Taylor expansion
\mathcal{T}_{R}[i]
.

(3)

We consider cardinalities of equivalence classes
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)}^{k}(\mathcal{K}_{k,j}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)))/\equiv
of
critical components
of
\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)
for the relation
\equiv
in order to deduce the cardinalities of equivalence classes
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[i+1](e)}^{k}(\mathcal{K}_{k,j}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i+1](e)))/\equiv
of
critical components
of
\mathcal{T}_{R}[i+1](e)
and the contents of the new boxes of depth
i
of
\mathcal{T}_{R}[i+1](e)
.

3.1.
The borders of the boxes

In this subsection we first show how to recover the set
\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}^{\geq i}(R)}\{\log_{k}(m);m\in e^{\#}(o)\}
and, therefore, the set
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\oc}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e))\setminus\mathcal{B}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e))
of co-contractions of
\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)
(Lemma
28
). Next, we show how to determine, from
\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)
, the set
\mathcal{B}_{0}^{=i}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i+1](e))
of “new” boxes and, for any such “new” box
o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{=i}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i+1](e))
, its
border
i.e. the set
t_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[i+1](e)}[\{o\}\times\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[i+1](e)}^{\mathsf{f}}(o)]
of exponential ports that are immediately below (Proposition
31
), which, as shown by Example
3.1.2
, p.
3.1.2
, is an information that is not provided by the LPS but is still too weak to rebuild the PS (the borders of the boxes do not allow to recover their outlines). In particular, we have
\mathcal{B}_{0}^{=i}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i+1](e))=!_{e,i}[\mathcal{N}_{i}(e)]
, where the set
\mathcal{N}_{i}(e)\subseteq\mathbb{N}
is defined from the set
\mathcal{M}_{0}(e)
of the numbers of copies of boxes taken by the pseudo–experiment
e
:

{defi}

Let
R
be a differential in-PS. Let
k>1
. Let
e
be a
k
-heterogeneous pseudo-experiment on
R
. For any
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we define, by induction on
i
,
\mathcal{M}_{i}(e)\subseteq\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
and
(m_{i,j}(e))_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\in\{0,\ldots,k-1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
as follows.
We set
\mathcal{M}_{0}(e)=\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}(R){}}\{j\in\mathbb{N};k^{j}\in e^{\#}(o)\}
and we write
\mathsf{Card}\left(\mathcal{M}_{i}(e)\right)
in base
k
:
\mathsf{Card}\left(\mathcal{M}_{i}(e)\right)=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}m_{i,j}(e)\cdot k^{j}
; we set
\mathcal{M}_{i+1}(e)=

\{j>0;m_{i,j}(e)\not=0\}
.

For any
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we set
\mathcal{N}_{i}(e)=\mathcal{M}_{i}(e)\setminus\mathcal{M}_{i+1}(e)
.

Notice that all the sets
\mathcal{M}_{i}(e)
and
\mathcal{N}_{i}(e)
can be computed from
\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e)
, since, by Lemma
24
, we have
\mathcal{M}_{0}(e)=

\log_{k}[\{{\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e)}(p);

p\in\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\oc}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[0](e))\}]
.

{exa}

If
e
is a
10
-heterogeneous pseudo-experiment as in Example
3
, then
\mathcal{M}_{0}(e)=\{1,\ldots,224\}
. We have
\mathsf{Card}\left(\mathcal{M}_{0}(e)\right)=4+2\cdot 10^{1}+2\cdot 10^{2}
, hence
\mathcal{M}_{1}(e)=\{1,2\}
and
\mathcal{N}_{0}(e)=\{3,\ldots,224\}
. We have
\mathsf{Card}\left(\mathcal{M}_{1}(e)\right)=2
, hence
\mathcal{M}_{2}(e)=\emptyset
and
\mathcal{N}_{1}(e)=\{1,2\}
.

Lemma 26
.

Let
k>1
. Let
R
be an in-PS. If
e
is a
k
-heterogeneous pseudo-experiment on
R
, then, for any
i\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a unique bijection

\oc_{e,i}:\log_{k}[\bigcup e^{\#}[\mathcal{B}^{\geq i}(R)]]\simeq\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\oc}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e))\setminus\mathcal{B}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e))

such that, for any
j\in\mathsf{dom}(\oc_{e,i})
, the following properties hold:

(1)

k^{j}\in(e^{\#}\circ\kappa_{R}[i](e)\circ\oc_{e,i})(j)

(2)

and
({\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)}\circ!_{e,i})(j)=k^{j}
.

Proof 3.4
.

Notice first that, for any
o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)
, we have
{\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)}(o)=\mathsf{Card}\left(e(o)\right)
, hence the function that associates with every
j\in\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)}\{\log_{k}(m);m\in e^{\#}(o)\}
the unique
o_{j}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)
such that
e^{\#}(o_{j})=\{k^{j}\}
is a bijection

\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)}\{\log_{k}(m);m\in e^{\#}(o)\}\simeq\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\oc}(R)\setminus\mathcal{B}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e))

such that
{\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)}(o_{j})=\sum e^{\#}(o_{j})=k^{j}
.

Now, we prove the lemma by induction on
\textit{depth}(R)
. We set

\oc_{e,i}(j)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}o_{j}&\textit{if $j\in\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)}\{\log_{k}(m);m\in e^{\#}(o)\}$;}\\
(o,(e_{o},\oc_{e_{o},i}(j)))&\textit{if $o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)$ and $k^{j}\in({e_{o}}^{\#}\circ\kappa_{e_{o},i}\circ\oc_{e_{o},i})(j)$.}\end{array}\right.

For checking Property
2
, notice that, for any
o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)
, for any
e_{o}\in e(o)
, for any
p\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o}))
, we have
{\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)}(o,(e_{o},p))={\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{B_{R}(o)}[i](e_{o})}(p)
.

3.1.1.
Identifying the co-contractions that correspond to the new boxes

This part is devoted to prove Lemma
28
, which shows that, for any
k
-heterogeneous pseudo-experiment
e
on
R
, for any
i\in\mathbb{N}
, the function
\oc_{e,i}
is actually a bijection
\mathcal{M}_{i}(e)\to\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\oc}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e))\setminus\mathcal{B}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e))
such that, for any
j\in\mathcal{M}_{i}(e)
, we have
({\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e)}\circ\oc_{e,i})(j)=k^{j}
.

Lemma 27
.

Let
k>1
. For any
i\in\mathbb{N}
, for any in-PS
R
such that
\mathsf{Card}\left(\mathcal{B}(R)\right)<k
, for any
k
-heterogeneous pseudo-experiment
e
on
R
, the following properties hold:

(1)

P_{1}(R,e,i)
:
(\forall o,o^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(R))(\forall e_{o}\in e(o))(\forall j\in\mathcal{M}_{i}(e_{o}))k^{j}\notin e^{\#}(o^{\prime})

(2)

P_{2}(R,e,i)
:
(\forall o,o^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(R))(\forall e_{o}\in e(o))(\forall e_{o^{\prime}}\in e(o^{\prime}))(\mathcal{M}_{i}(e_{o})\cap\mathcal{M}_{i}(e_{o^{\prime}})\not=\emptyset\Rightarrow(o,e_{o})=(o^{\prime},e_{o^{\prime}}))

(3)

P_{3}(R,e,i)
:
(\textit{depth}(R)=i\Rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{i}(e)=\emptyset)

(4)

P_{4}(R,e,i)
:
m_{i,0}(e)=\mathsf{Card}\left(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)\right)

(5)

P_{5}(R,e,i)
:
\mathcal{M}_{i}(e)\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{i-1}(e)

(6)

P_{6}(R,e,i)
:
\mathcal{M}_{i}(e)=\log_{k}[\bigcup e^{\#}[\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)]]\cup\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i+1}(R)}\bigcup_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\mathcal{M}_{i}(e_{o})

Proof 3.5
.

By complete induction on
\textit{depth}(R)+i
. First, if
i=0=\textit{depth}(R)
, then
\mathcal{M}_{-1}=\emptyset=\mathcal{M}_{0}
, hence
P_{1}(R,e,i)
,
P_{2}(R,e,i)
,
P_{3}(R,e,i)
,
P_{4}(R,e,i)
,
P_{5}(R,e,i)
and
P_{6}(R,e,i)
hold trivially. Now, let
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Notice that if
P_{6}(R,e,i)
holds, then
P_{3}(R,e,i)
holds; moreover, if, in addition,
P_{1}(R,e,i)
and
P_{2}(R,e,i)
hold, then

\displaystyle\mathsf{Card}\left(\mathcal{M}_{i}(e)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsf{Card}\left(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)\right)+\sum_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i+1}(R)}\sum_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\mathsf{Card}\left(\mathcal{M}_{i}(e_{o})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsf{Card}\left(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)\right)+\sum_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i+1}(R)}\sum_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{M}_{i+1}(e_{o})\cup\{0\}}m_{i,j}(e_{o})\cdot k^{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsf{Card}\left(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i+1}(R)}\left(\mathsf{Card}\left(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(B_{R}(o))\right)\cdot\mathsf{Card}\left(e(o)\right)+\sum_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{M}_{i+1}(e_{o})}m_{i,j}(e_{o})\cdot k^{j}\right)

(by
P_{4}(B_{R}(o),e_{o},i)
for each
o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i+1}(R)
and each
e_{o}\in e(o)
)

We thus have
P_{4}(R,e,i)
. Moreover, if
i>0
, then by applying
P_{6}(R,e,i-1)
,
P_{6}(R,e,i)
and
P_{5}(B_{R}(o),e_{o},i)
for each
o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i+1}(R)
and each
e_{o}\in o
, and by noticing the inclusion
\log_{k}[\bigcup e^{\#}[\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)]]\subseteq\log_{k}[\bigcup e^{\#}[\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i-1}(R)]]
, we obtain
P_{5}(R,e,i)
.

•

Let us assume that
i=0
. For any in-PS
R
, for any
k
-heterogeneous pseudo-experiment
e
on
R
, since
\bigcup e^{\#}[\mathcal{B}(R)]\subseteq\{k^{j};j>0\}
, we have
\mathsf{Card}\left(\mathcal{M}_{-1}(e)\right)=\sum\bigcup e^{\#}[\mathcal{B}(R)]=\sum_{j\in\log_{k}[\bigcup e^{\#}[\mathcal{B}(R)]]}k^{j}
, hence
\mathcal{M}_{0}(e)=\log_{k}[\bigcup e^{\#}[\mathcal{B}(R)]]
.

Let
R
be an in-PS such that
\mathsf{Card}\left(\mathcal{B}(R)\right)<k
and let
e
be a
k
-heterogeneous experiment on
R
.

Let
o,o^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(R)
and let
e_{o}\in e(o)
. Let
j\in\mathcal{M}_{0}(e_{o})
. There exists
o^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{B}(B_{R}(o))
such that
k^{j}\in{e_{o}}^{\#}(o^{\prime\prime})
. Assume that
k^{j}\in e^{\#}(o^{\prime})
. We have
e^{\#}((o,o^{\prime\prime}))\cap e^{\#}(o^{\prime})\not=\emptyset
; but, by the definition of
k
-heterogeneous experiment
(Definition
3
), this entails that
o^{\prime}=(o,o^{\prime\prime})
; we thus obtain a contradiction with the following requirement of the definition of in-PS’s (Definition
1.1
): for any
(p_{1},p_{2})\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(R)
, we have
p_{1}\notin\mathcal{B}_{0}(R)
. We showed that
P_{1}(R,e,0)
holds.

Let
o,o^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(R)
. Let
e_{o}\in e(o)
and
e_{o^{\prime}}\in e(o^{\prime})
such that
\mathcal{M}_{0}(e_{o})\cap\mathcal{M}_{0}(e_{o^{\prime}})\not=\emptyset
. Let
j\in\mathcal{M}_{0}(e_{o})\cap\mathcal{M}_{0}(e_{o^{\prime}})
. There exist
o_{1}\in\mathcal{B}(B_{R}(o))
such that
k^{j}\in{e_{o}}^{\#}(o_{1})=e^{\#}(o,o_{1})
and
o_{2}\in\mathcal{B}(B_{R}(o^{\prime}))
such that
k^{j}\in{e_{o^{\prime}}}^{\#}(o_{2})=e^{\#}(o^{\prime},o_{2})
. By the definition of
k
-heterogeneous experiment
, we have
(o,o_{1})=(o^{\prime},o_{2})
. We have
{e_{o}}^{\#}(o_{1})\cap{e_{o^{\prime}}}^{\#}(o_{1})\not=\emptyset
, hence, again by the definition of
k
-heterogeneous experiment
, we obtain
e_{o}=e_{o^{\prime}}
. We showed that
P_{2}(R,e,0)
holds.

We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{0}(e)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log_{k}[\bigcup e^{\#}[\mathcal{B}(R)]]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log_{k}[\bigcup e^{\#}[\mathcal{B}_{0}(R)]]\cup\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq 1}(R)}\log_{k}[\bigcup e^{\#}[\{o\}\times\mathcal{B}_{0}(B_{R}(o))]]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log_{k}[\bigcup e^{\#}[\mathcal{B}_{0}(R)]]\cup\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq 1}(R)}\bigcup_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\log_{k}[\bigcup{e_{o}}^{\#}[\mathcal{B}_{0}(B_{R}(o))]]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log_{k}[\bigcup e^{\#}[\mathcal{B}_{0}(R)]]\cup\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq 1}(R)}\bigcup_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\mathcal{M}_{0}(e_{o})

hence
P_{6}(R,e,0)
holds. We already know that it follows that
P_{4}(R,e,0)
holds.

Let
j\in\mathcal{M}_{0}(e)
: We have
k^{j}\in\bigcup e^{\#}[\mathcal{B}(R)]
, hence
k^{j}\leq\sum\bigcup e^{\#}[\mathcal{B}(R)]
. Since
j<k^{j}
, we have
j<\sum\bigcup e^{\#}[\mathcal{B}(R)]
; so
j\in\mathcal{M}_{-1}(e)
. We thus proved
P_{5}(R,e,0)
.

•

Let us assume that
i>0
. Let
R
be an in-PS such that
\mathsf{Card}\left(\mathcal{B}(R)\right)<k
and let
e
be a
k
-heterogeneous experiment on
R
. By
P_{1}(R,e,i-1)
and
P_{5}(R,e,i-1)
, we have
P_{1}(R,e,i)
. By
P_{2}(R,e,i-1)
and
P_{5}(R,e,i-1)
, we have
P_{2}(R,e,i)
.
We have

\displaystyle\mathsf{Card}\left(\mathcal{M}_{i-1}(e)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsf{Card}\left(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i-1}(R)\right)+\sum_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)}\sum_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\mathsf{Card}\left(\mathcal{M}_{i-1}(e_{o})\right)

(by
P_{6}(R,e,i-1)
,
P_{1}(R,e,i-1)
and
P_{2}(R,e,i-1)
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsf{Card}\left(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i-1}(R)\right)+\sum_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)}\sum_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\left(m_{i-1,0}(e_{o})+\sum_{j\in\mathcal{M}_{i}(e_{o})}m_{i-1,j}(e_{o})\cdot k^{j}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsf{Card}\left(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i-1}(R)\right)+\sum_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)}\sum_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\left(\mathsf{Card}\left(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i-1}(B_{R}(o))\right)+\sum_{j\in\mathcal{M}_{i}(e_{o})}m_{i-1,j}(e_{o})\cdot k^{j}\right)

(by
P_{4}(B_{R}(o),e_{o},i-1)
for each
o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)
and each
e_{o}\in e(o)
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsf{Card}\left(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i-1}(R)\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)}\left(\mathsf{Card}\left(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i-1}(B_{R}(o))\right)\cdot\mathsf{Card}\left(e(o)\right)+\sum_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{M}_{i}(e_{o})}m_{i-1,j}(e_{o})\cdot k^{j}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsf{Card}\left(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i-1}(R)\right)+\sum_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)}\mathsf{Card}\left(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i-1}(B_{R}(o))\right)\cdot\sum e^{\#}(o)

\displaystyle+\sum_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i+1}(R)}\sum_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{M}_{i}(e_{o})}m_{i-1,j}(e_{o})\cdot k^{j}

(by
P_{3}(B_{R}(o),e_{o},i)
for each
o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{=i}(R)
and each
e_{o}\in e(o)
)

By
P_{1}(R,e,i)
and
P_{2}(R,e,i)
, we have
\{j>0;m_{i-1,j}(e)\not=0\}=\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)}\{j\in\mathbb{N};k^{j}=\sum e^{\#}(o)\}\cup\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i+1}(R)}\bigcup_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\mathcal{M}_{i}(e_{o})
, hence
P_{6}(R,e,i)
holds.

Lemma 28
.

Let
R
be an in-PS.
Let
k>\mathsf{Card}\left(\mathcal{B}(R){}\right)
.
For any
k
-heterogeneous pseudo-experiment
e
on
R
, for any
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
\mathcal{M}_{i}(e)=\log_{k}[\bigcup e^{\#}[\mathcal{B}^{\geq i}(R)]]
, hence
\mathcal{N}_{i}(e)=\log_{k}[\bigcup e^{\#}[\mathcal{B}^{=i}(R)]]
and
(\kappa_{R}[i](e)\circ\oc_{e,i})[\mathcal{N}_{i}(e)]=\mathcal{B}^{=i}(R)
.

Proof 3.6
.

By induction on
\textit{depth}(R)
. We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{i}(e)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log_{k}[\bigcup e^{\#}[\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)]]\cup\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i+1}(R)}\bigcup_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\mathcal{M}_{i}(e_{o})

(by Lemma
27
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log_{k}[\bigcup e^{\#}[\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)]]\cup\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i+1}(R)}\bigcup_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\log_{k}[\bigcup{e_{o}}^{\#}[\mathcal{B}^{\geq i}(B_{R}(o))]]

(by the induction hypothesis)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log_{k}[\bigcup e^{\#}[\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)]\cup\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i+1}(R)}\bigcup_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\bigcup{e_{o}}^{\#}[\mathcal{B}^{\geq i}(B_{R}(o))]]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log_{k}[\bigcup e^{\#}[\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)]\cup\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i+1}(R)}\bigcup_{e_{o}\in e(o)}\bigcup{e}^{\#}[\{o\}\times\mathcal{B}^{\geq i}(B_{R}(o))]]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log_{k}[\bigcup e^{\#}[\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i}(R)\cup\bigcup_{o\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\geq i+1}(R)}\bigcup_{e_{o}\in e(o)}(\{o\}\times\mathcal{B}^{\geq i}(B_{R}(o)))]]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log_{k}[\bigcup e^{\#}[\mathcal{B}^{\geq i}(R)]]

Hence

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{i}(e)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{i}(e)\setminus\mathcal{M}_{i+1}(e)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log_{k}[\bigcup e^{\#}[\mathcal{B}^{\geq i}(R)]]\setminus\log_{k}[\bigcup e^{\#}[\mathcal{B}^{\geq i+1}(R)]]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log_{k}[\bigcup e^{\#}[\mathcal{B}^{=i}(R)]]

Moreover, since
(\forall j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}(e))k^{j}\in(e^{\#}\circ\kappa_{R}[i](e)\circ\oc_{e,i})(j)
, we obtain
(\kappa_{R}[i](e)\circ\oc_{e,i})[\mathcal{N}_{i}(e)]=\mathcal{B}^{=i}(R)
.

{exa}

(Continuation of Example
3.1
) We thus have
\mathcal{M}_{1}(e)=\{1,2\}
and
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\oc}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e))\setminus\mathcal{B}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[1](e))=\linebreak\{o_{2},o_{4}\}
with
{\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[1](e)}(o_{2})

=

10^{1}
and
{\textit{a}}_{\mathcal{T}_{R}[1](e)}(o_{4})

=

10^{2}
(see Figures
18
,
18
and
18
- we recall that
\mathcal{T}_{R}[1](e)=S1_{1}\oplus S1_{2}\oplus S1_{3}
).

3.1.2.
Determining the contractions immediately below the new boxes

The set
\mathcal{K}_{k,\mathcal{N}_{i}(e)}(S)
of “critical ports” is a set of exponential ports that will play a crucial role in our algorithm.

{defi}

Let
S
be a differential in-PS. Let
k>1
. For any
p\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(S)
, we define the sequence
(m_{k,j}(S)(p))_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\in\{0,\ldots,k-1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
as follows:
{\textit{a}}_{S}(p)=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}m_{k,j}(S)(p)\cdot k^{j}
. For any
j\in\mathbb{N}
, we set
\mathcal{K}_{k,j}(S)=

\{p\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(S);m_{k,j}(S)(p)\not=0\}\cap\mathcal{P}^{\textit{e}}(\mathcal{G}(S))
and, for any
J\subseteq\mathbb{N}
, we set
\mathcal{K}_{k,J}(S)=

\bigcup_{j\in J}\mathcal{K}_{k,j}(S)
.

In particular, for any
j\in\mathcal{M}_{i}(e)
, we have
\oc_{e,i}(j)\in\mathcal{K}_{k,j}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[i](e))
.

{exa}

We have
\mathcal{K}_{10,1}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[1](e))=\{p_{1},p_{4},p_{5},p_{6},p_{7},o_{2}\}
and
\mathcal{K}_{10,2}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[1](e))=\{p_{4},

p_{5},

p_{6},

p_{7},

o_{4}\}
, where
\mathcal{T}_{R}[1](e)=S1_{1}\oplus S1_{2}\oplus S1_{3}
with
S1_{1}
,
S1_{2}
and
S1_{3}
depicted in Figures
18
,
18
and
18
respectively. So we have
\mathcal{K}_{10,\{1,2\}}(\mathcal{T}_{R}[1](e))=\{p_{1},p_{4},p_{5},p_{6},p_{7},o_{2},o_{4}\}
.

Critical ports are defined by their arities. Proposition
31
shows that they are exponential ports that are immediately below the “new” boxes.

In particular, this proposition highlights one more essential difference between the
k
-experiments of
[
34
,
35
,
13
,
20
]
and our
k
-heterogeneous experiments. There, such a
k
-experiment labelling some contraction
p
with a multiset of cardinality
\sum_{j}m_{j}\cdot k^{j}
(where
0\leq m_{j}<k
for any
j
) gives the information that immediately above the contraction
p
there are exactly
m_{j_{0}}
series of exactly
j_{0}
auxiliary doors. Here, whenever a
k
-heterogeneous experiment labels some contraction
p
with a multiset of cardinality
\sum_{j}m_{j}\cdot k^{j}
(where
0\leq m_{j}<k
for any
j
), the integer
j_{0}
is not related to the number of auxiliary doors in series anymore; it corresponds, in the case
m_{j_{0}}>0
, with the existence of a box that has an occurrence taking
k^{j_{0}}
copies of its content, the box having, among all its auxiliary doors, exactly
m_{j_{0}}
auxiliary doors that are, each of them,
the first one
(i.e. the deepest one) of a series of auxiliary doors immediately above the contraction
p
.

By the way, the constraints on the experiments are completely different: The constraints in
[
34
,
35
,
13
,
20
]
give a lower bound on the arities of the co-contractions, while the constraints here are on the
basis

k
. For instance, in the case of Figure
21
, the co-size is
<100
and there are only
2
boxes, but still it is not enough to consider a
10
-heterogeneous experiments with powers
\geq 2
: by taking an experiment
e
with
e^{\#}(o_{1})=10^{3}
and
e^{\#}(o_{2})=10^{2}
, we get a contraction of arity
9800=9\cdot 10^{3}+8\cdot 10^{2}
in the corresponding term of the Taylor expansion, which, following our decomposition, would correspond to the PS of Figure
21
.
